# SOL

## Lithic Manuscript 2

### Draft Version 1.0

---

## Prologue - The Snow After the Flash

### 2079

The second time they burned a door, Nadiya made them vote.

It had been a conference-room door before the Exchange, walnut over a fireproof core, selected by an administrator who believed authority should sound heavy when it closed. The veneer would burn. The core would not. Darius Cole stood over it with a pry bar and explained this three times while forty-two residents watched from the archive corridor.

"We strip it first," he said. "Then it is fuel."

The records custodian held the ration ledger against his chest. "The conference room is assigned to two families."

"A blanket keeps out as much noise as that door does."

"That is not the point."

"The point is heat."

Nadiya sat at a folding table between them. Her left hand had gone stiff from cold. She kept it beneath her coat and wrote with her right.

"The families get another partition," she said.

"From where?" the custodian asked.

"That is the motion."

Darius looked at the ceiling. "We are voting on a door."

"We are voting on whether two families lose one."

"Everyone loses heat if they keep it."

"Then the vote should be easy."

It was not.

The two families had seven people between them. One included a nursing mother. The other included a machinist who worked nights on the failing heat exchanger and slept through the day. A blanket partition would admit the common corridor's light, arguments, coughs, and cooking smoke. The residents near the conference room wanted the door burned because they felt the cold under their bedding. Residents farther from it wanted the executive desks burned first, although those desks were laminated composite and produced black smoke.

They argued for twenty-six minutes.

The walnut veneer gave them less than an hour of useful heat.

Nadiya recorded the motion, objections, vote, substitute partition, and review date. When she finished, Darius had already driven the pry bar under the first hinge.

"You know the door doesn't care," he said.

"The families do."

"The cold doesn't."

"That is why the cold cannot chair the meeting."

Darius snorted. He had stopped laughing easily after the thaw exposed the archive's sanitation failures, but irritation could still reach him. "Put that in your book."

"I just did."

The archive had kept records before it kept people. Now every storage level had cots, cook drums, drying lines, waste routes, and arguments about what records were worth the space they occupied. The central vault still held the Varga atlas, Leila Sayegh's medical papers, the Separation Testimony Eve Mercer had given Nadiya, and enough contradictory national archives to prevent any one survivor from claiming innocence without effort. Less valuable holdings migrated toward the walls. Duplicate procurement records became insulation. Empty shelving became stove fuel. Furniture became splints, bunks, braces, lids, and heat.

The institution survived by consuming its previous purpose.

Nadiya did not write that sentence down. It sounded too complete.

Rami Vale watched Darius remove the door. He was nine years old by the birth record his mother had carried in a plastic sleeve and perhaps ten by her memory. The registry kept both dates. Since the Exchange, he had grown two centimeters and lost three kilograms. He had also become the archive's most persistent map critic.

He carried the blue pencil in a length of cloth tied around his neck.

"If they burn all the doors," he asked, "how will we know where rooms end?"

Darius nodded toward the concrete walls. "Those help."

"What if they burn the walls?"

"Then we have a different problem."

Rami considered the answer. "You say that when you don't know."

"That's one use for it."

Nadiya added the phrase to the margin of her ledger before she could decide why.

Outside, snow fell through soot and came down gray. It collected against the intake sheds, the covered vehicles, the radio mast, and Paul's air-filter grave marker. No one allowed children outdoors alone. Wind rearranged contaminated drifts, and the ground still held fragments of roofs, glass, wire, and people.

The radio room remained the archive's only convincing proof that distance had not become emptiness.

Most transmissions were automated. Chicago repeated tunnel temperatures and switch-part requests. An Ubuntu agricultural relay broadcast fungal-resistance instructions, then went silent for six days and returned with half its languages missing. A Yamama civil station sent water inventories at dawn and burial totals at night. Europolis trains announced arrivals at platforms buried under collapsed stations. From CRINK territory came reactor warnings without coordinates.

Human voices appeared between those routines and vanished before questions returned.

Nadiya scheduled listening shifts as if attention could create continuity. Every operator wrote down time, frequency, language, signal strength, claims, and uncertainty. They did not record "nothing." They recorded static because static had properties.

On the night after the door vote, the signal arrived at 02:13.

"North municipal clinic to any receiving shelter. North municipal clinic. Generator loss. Neonatal ward occupied. Four infants, three adults. Interior temperature eleven and falling. Road approach blocked east. Repeat, road approach blocked east."

The voice paused.

"We can hear something in the service level."

The operator woke Nadiya because the clinic lay within the archive's old municipal map. He woke Darius because every rescue now required someone who understood contaminated buildings. He woke Iris Bell-Reyes because four infants turned a radio message into a medical request whether the speaker used the word or not.

They gathered over the map table.

The clinic was nineteen kilometers away by the old road and perhaps thirty by usable routes. The archive had one tracked utility carrier capable of making the journey. Its batteries could survive the cold if they warmed them first. The warming cycle would consume enough electricity to power the isolation ward for seven hours.

"Four infants," Iris said.

"And sixty-three people here," the custodian replied.

"I can count."

"The ward has two oxygen-dependent patients."

"I know their names."

Darius examined the map. "East road is gone at the underpass. We use the tram cut."

Nadiya drew the proposed route in pencil. "The cut flooded during spring thaw."

"It froze."

"That does not tell us what froze inside it."

"It tells us we can drive on top."

The radio operator had called the clinic three times. No response.

Iris pulled her sleeves over her hands. "The message could be old."

"Timestamp is current," the operator said.

"The transmitter's timestamp is current. The voice could be stored."

"Why would it store that?"

"Why did a hospital intercom call rooms after the rooms were demolished?" Iris asked. "Old systems repeat. Wetwire repeated. People program distress loops. Pick one."

Wetwire was the survivor name for an ecology nobody had successfully reduced to one origin.

It was not a plague that raised the dead. It was not software that became conscious. The strongest pre-Exchange model described a convergence of neural scaffolds, persistent tissue cultures, conductive polymers, livestock contamination, recycled medical devices, and the waste systems that had carried all of them through the same buildings.

Wetwire grew where protein, water, heat, conductive material, and repeated mechanical rhythm met. It could use scar tissue, insulation, buried cable, pumps, animal implants, service cavities, and hospital waste as parts of one habitat. Most colonies did not move. They preserved patterns.

An elevator continued answering calls after its controls were cut.

An intercom replayed a nurse's voice.

A pump altered its cycle after responders entered.

Investigators said the nest had entrained itself to routine. Survivors said the building had learned them. Neither explanation made a false distress call easier to ignore.

The operator looked at the speaker.

Darius tapped the route. "We take the carrier, portable heat, two sealed cradles. Five people."

"Six," Iris said.

"Five fits with equipment."

"Four infants do not fit two cradles."

"Four infants are a claim on a radio."

Iris turned toward him. "You think they're dead."

"I think the building has had six days without reported traffic, the east road is blocked, and whoever transmitted heard something in the service level."

"That was a Wetwire phrase fifteen years ago."

"It was a janitor phrase before that."

Nadiya listened while each of them became more certain from the same absence.

The archive's rescue rule required a public accounting when a mission risked common life-support capacity. It was a rule Nadiya had helped write after a scavenging team took the only functioning radiation meter without telling anyone. She had believed public accounting would make risk legible.

At 03:05, sixty-three tired people assembled near the warm trunk to decide whether strangers might be alive.

The machinist from behind the conference-room door opposed the mission. His wife supported it. A former soldier demanded a thermal drone survey, although the archive's last drone had not returned from an intake inspection. Paul's husband asked whether the clinic kept antibiotics. Three residents changed their position when told it might. One changed back when Iris explained that neonatal medicine might have no general use.

Rami sat near Nadiya's feet.

"Did you vote before you came for us?" he asked.

Nadiya remembered the vehicle bay, the children between racks, and the old world ending faster than any procedure could follow.

"No."

"Why now?"

"Because now we have time."

"They don't."

The answer spread through the room more efficiently than anything Nadiya had said.

The vote authorized the carrier by four.

Nadiya recorded the result. She did not record that a child's interruption had altered it. There was no field for that.

Darius found her at the vehicle bay while Iris heated chemical packs inside her coat.

"Your ledger made that look cleaner than it was," he said.

"It records the decision."

"It records hands."

"Those hands decided."

"The boy decided. Half the room was deciding whether they wanted clinic drugs."

"Motives are not countable."

"Neither are people in a radio message, apparently."

Nadiya closed the ledger. "What do you want me to write?"

"That we did not know. That we went anyway."

"I always write uncertainty."

"You put it in a column. People put columns in uniforms later."

She wanted to tell him that records restrained uniforms. She had spent her life preserving evidence because secrecy let institutions convert doubt into authority. Yet the archive now used her records to assign food, beds, work, medicine, rescue, and punishment. Every review improved the process and made the archive more capable of governing.

"Bring them back," she said.

Darius lifted a sealed tool case into the carrier. "That's not a recordkeeping instruction."

"No."

"Good."

The carrier left before dawn, if dawn still meant anything. Its work lights passed through the outer gate and became two pale stains in the snow.

The archive waited.

At breakfast, residents complained that the porridge had absorbed smoke from the walnut veneer. The machinist slept behind the new blanket partition with cloth wrapped around his head. His wife sat beside the radio. Children attended lessons because keeping them near the warm trunk had acquired a curriculum.

Rami drew the clinic route in blue.

"You marked the old road," Nadiya said.

"That's what the map says."

"Darius used the tram cut."

"Then the map is wrong."

"The road still exists."

"Not if nobody can go there."

He rubbed at the line with his thumb. The pencil did not erase cleanly. A blue stain remained beneath the new route.

"There," he said. "Now it shows both."

At 13:40, the carrier transmitted once.

*Reached structure. No exterior movement. Entering west service door.*

At 15:02, the archive heard three seconds of breathing and a metal impact.

At 16:19, the radio emitted a neonatal oxygen alarm. Iris had warned them that nests once imitated routine sounds. The alarm repeated every forty-seven seconds, too regular to be human and too familiar to ignore.

At 17:31, the carrier returned.

People gathered at the gate until Nadiya forced them behind the marked line. The vehicle's hull was streaked with black slush. One forward light had broken. Darius climbed out first and removed his outer gloves before touching anything clean.

Iris appeared behind him carrying a bundled infant against her chest.

Only one.

The clinic's neonatal ward had held four incubators. Three were empty. The transmitting nurse had moved the surviving child into a records room because it had no ventilation grille. She had died beside the door after programming the distress loop. The other adults were found in the stairwell. The service level contained no active Wetwire, only a burst pipe knocking against a cable tray.

"What about the other infants?" someone asked.

Iris looked at the crowd. "We don't know they were ever there."

"The message said four."

"The board listed four. The board may not have been updated."

"So we risked the ward for one?"

Darius stepped between Iris and the speaker. "We went for a claim. We returned with a child."

The child was a girl, six weeks old according to a paper chart and nine according to the date printed by a machine whose calendar had reset twice. Her name band read LINA QURESHI. The archive registry kept the name, both ages, the nurse's statement, and a blank space for family.

That night, the residents voted to reduce common heat for six hours to stabilize the isolation ward.

No one argued for twenty-six minutes.

Rami stood beside the observation window when the soot thinned enough to reveal a pale disc behind the cloud. The adults stopped in the corridor. Some crossed themselves. Some whispered prayers. One woman cursed because everyone had blocked the route to the latrine.

Rami pressed his fingers against the glass.

"Is that the sun?"

Nadiya joined him.

It looked nothing like the yellow circle children drew. It had no rays. It offered almost no warmth. The damaged filters darkened it further, and smoke from distant settlements smudged the horizon.

"Yes," she said.

"It looks sick."

"It may look different tomorrow."

"Better?"

Nadiya had taught him to separate observation from claim. She had also watched adults use uncertainty as permission to promise anything.

"Different," she said.

Rami removed the blue pencil from his neck and drew a small circle in the margin of the clinic map.

Below it he wrote, in the uneven hand of a child who had learned maps before sunlight:

*Seen from here.*

The Exchange changed every faith that survived it.

No tradition emerged from the shelters with all its old answers intact. Scriptures remained. Prayers remained. Lineages of interpretation remained wherever teachers, families, recordings, or damaged books preserved them. Certainty did not.

Survivors asked why civilization had continued.

Some answered providence. Others refused to call unequal survival divine selection. Some called survival duty, accident, karma, consequence, mercy, attachment, or a question whose answer could become dangerous if it made the living feel chosen over the dead.

At the Sayegh Archive, a Christian chaplain removed the phrase *spared for a purpose* from a burial service after a mother asked what purpose had killed her other children.

A Muslim physician wrote provisional guidance for prayer, fasting, burial, and communal obligation when clocks failed, clean water was rationed, and no recognized authority could answer before morning.

A Jewish records keeper began copying family names into the margin of a damaged legal text because the community had lost the separate registry.

A Hindu engineer argued with the heat council for enough current to maintain one sealed ceremonial lamp and then surrendered the allocation when the neonatal room cooled.

A Buddhist counselor taught breathing in a corridor where every breath also consumed measured air. She refused to describe panic as attachment while the ventilation alarm sounded.

An atheist machinist attended every burial and denied that attendance constituted belief.

None abandoned the traditions named on their old records.

None practiced them exactly as before.

The first interfaith meeting at the archive did not seek doctrinal unity. It began because burial cloth, clean water, fire, quiet rooms, calendar time, and body handling all competed with survival systems.

Nadiya gave each community one sheet of paper.

"Write what cannot be surrendered," she said.

The Christian group returned three incompatible sheets.

The Muslim group requested time for consultation.

The Jewish group wrote questions in the margin before answering.

The Hindu households asked whether the paper represented one tradition or many.

The Buddhist residents returned a nearly blank page with a note that silence still required a warm room.

The secular group objected to being described as one group at all.

Darius looked at the pile.

"Useful?"

"Accurate," Nadiya said.

The meeting produced no common theology. It produced shared obligations.

No survivor would be denied water for refusing a rite.

No burial record would declare a soul's status.

No faith could claim emergency authority over another household.

Every community could preserve names, mourn its dead, teach its children, and petition for the material required to practice.

The questions outlived the emergency:

Why had civilization survived?

What did survival obligate?

How should rebuilding differ from restoring the conditions that had failed?

Later generations added questions the shelters could not yet imagine.

Was expansion into space calling, duty, or necessity?

Did a life extended for centuries alter the soul, or only the responsibilities of the person living it?

Every major religion answered differently within itself.

The Exchange did not produce one new faith.

It made unchanged faith impossible.

In 2080, the archive ran out of furniture it could burn without losing beds.

The shortage announced itself as a clean patch on a wall. Darius removed the last executive bookshelf, split its laminated sides with a wedge, and carried the pieces to the warm trunk. Behind it, the paint had never yellowed. Residents stopped in the corridor to look at the pale rectangle as though another door had opened.

That evening, the heat ledger showed thirty-six hours of fuel.

Nadiya walked every occupied level with the inventory. Tables had already become bunks. Cabinet doors had become toilet screens and stretcher boards. Chair legs reinforced the failing intake frame. Empty shelves held medical supplies because cardboard softened in the damp. The archive still contained wood, but now it contained words too.

She called the vote beside the warm trunk.

Residents arrived carrying blankets and cups. Children sat closest to the heat. Behind Nadiya, four carts held the first proposed burn list: duplicate engineering manuals, incomplete encyclopedias, telephone directories from cities that no longer answered, superseded procurement binders, and popular novels represented by more than one surviving copy.

No unique record had been listed. No medical text had been listed. No book for which the archive held only one copy had been listed.

That did not make the carts look harmless.

Darius lifted a telephone directory. Its pages had swollen from damp until the cover bowed.

"No one needs fourteen kilograms of dead numbers."

Rami reached for it. "The names aren't dead."

"Can you call them?"

"No."

"Can you find them?"

"Maybe."

Nadiya took the directory from Darius and opened it. Ink had transferred between pages. Half the addresses belonged to streets that were now craters, flood channels, or unmarked routes through snow.

"It is one copy of seven overlapping directories," she said.

"Overlapping isn't duplicate," Rami replied.

The residents began arguing before she finished the inventory.

An engineer defended obsolete turbine manuals because old settlements still operated obsolete turbines. A teacher defended the encyclopedias because children needed information no surviving adult thought to teach. A burial clerk defended the directories as population evidence. The kitchen staff asked which of those books would keep the nursery above eight degrees.

Iris stood near the isolation-room door with both hands inside her sleeves.

"Cold injury admissions doubled this week," she said. "The youngest patient is three."

The engineer held up a manual. "Knowledge is heat tomorrow."

From the floor, Lina Qureshi's night nurse answered, "Children freeze tonight."

Nobody applauded. The two sentences were not opponents. That made the decision worse.

Nadiya divided the vote into categories. First, duplicate records whose counterpart had been inspected and sealed. Second, damaged volumes whose legible information had been copied. Third, works held elsewhere according to radio confirmation. Darius objected that radio confirmation did not make a distant book reachable. Rami objected that copying a title did not copy marginal notes. Iris objected that every hour of review consumed the heat they were deciding how to preserve.

"Then vote on the first cart," Nadiya said.

The first cart passed.

Nobody moved it.

Darius finally took hold of the handle. Rami took the other side, not to help but to prevent him from moving too quickly. They wheeled it to the warm trunk together.

The first book burned badly. Its coated cover curled and smoked until the intake alarm sounded. Darius pulled it out, stripped the cover, and fed the pages in handfuls. Telephone-directory paper caught faster. Names blackened at the edges, lifted in the draft, and became heat against the children's faces.

Nadiya recorded every volume before it entered the fire. Title. Edition. Condition. Surviving counterpart. Vote authorization. Person who verified the record.

At midnight, Rami brought her a half-burned directory page that had escaped the grate.

"You missed this."

Nadiya read the surviving column of names.

"Put it in the burn record."

"It isn't a book now."

"It is evidence of one."

They called the night the First Library Fire, although the fire had burned for years and the library remained. The name made the decision sound singular. It was not. Every winter produced another list, another threshold, another argument about whether survival meant preserving knowledge or living long enough to use it.

No side won.

The nursery reached ten degrees.

---

# Part I - After the Fire

## Chapter 1 - Megacity Remnants

### 2084

The tower had been built for people who expected water to climb fifty-eight floors because they paid for it.

Six years after the Exchange, Darius stood in its lobby and watched meltwater descend the elevator cables.

Each drop struck the roof of Car Six with a hollow, irregular tap. The car had stopped level with the lobby, doors open, interior stripped. Someone had removed its copper control bus, wall panels, emergency battery, handrail, and floor tiles. A child's boot remained beneath the rear bench because no one wanted to reach far enough into the dark to retrieve it.

"Warm," said the inspection apprentice.

Darius looked at her.

She touched the elevator wall with the back of her glove. "Warmer than ambient."

"Do not touch unknown wet surfaces."

"It's my glove."

"Your glove travels with you."

She removed her hand.

The apprentice's name was Neve Okonkwo. She was nineteen, old enough to remember rain before the Exchange and young enough to treat that memory as evidence she understood weather. Her mother ran a fungal kitchen in the archive settlement. Neve wanted to be an infrastructure inspector because inspectors received first claim on recovered boots.

Darius wanted inspectors who noticed warmth.

He passed a sensor wand over the elevator opening. Temperature, humidity, ionizing radiation, volatile organics, conductive filaments. The wand showed nothing beyond rust, mold, and water two degrees warmer than the lobby.

"Machine room is above us," Neve said. "Could be residual heat."

"From what?"

"Battery bank."

"Five years dead?"

"Chemical decomposition."

"Good. Or?"

She looked up the shaft. "Someone is running power."

"Or?"

"Wetwire."

"Or?"

Neve's patience thinned. "Do you want every possible answer or the right one?"

"I want you alive long enough to learn the difference."

He marked the elevator bank yellow and moved on.

Outside, the megacity continued in pieces.

Three towers held permanent populations. Two more were seasonal salvage barracks. A buried transit station housed the heat plant, school, clinic, ration office, and market. Habitable corridors had been enclosed with concrete block, vehicle armor, old glass, and sections of anti-Wetwire wall dismantled from a military depot. Above the fourth floor, wind moved through broken windows and carried ash into rooms where office chairs remained arranged around tables.

Anti-Wetwire walls were not solid barriers alone. They layered dry gaps, visible inspection channels, sacrificial conduit, disconnected utilities, and materials with nothing useful to feed. Their purpose was to prevent one damp wall from becoming tissue, signal path, water route, and shelter at the same time.

The settlement called itself Lake Junction because the old municipal district name had been impossible to agree on. Half the surviving signs used another language. None pointed toward a lake anymore.

People had first come for the transit tunnels. The rails offered straight paths beneath rubble, and a cold-fusion maintenance cell beneath the station still produced enough power for pumps, lights, and air handling. Then they discovered the deep water mains had not cracked everywhere. Then traders came. Then families arrived and asked where children could sleep.

By 2084, Lake Junction had 8,900 registered residents, perhaps two thousand unregistered residents, six elected ward delegates, a heat authority, a salvage court, a burial slope, and no settled answer to who owned the buildings.

The salvage court existed because a copper thief had asked one useful question.

Three months earlier, transit guards had stopped Neve with forty meters of copper bus wrapped around her torso beneath a pressure coat. She had taken it from the twenty-third floor of a tower with no residents, no heat seal, and no registered claimant.

The guards called it theft because the city needed copper.

Neve called it salvage for the same reason.

At the hearing, Basma placed the coil on a table between them. Meltwater dripped from its insulation into a cooking pan. The tower's former corporate owner existed only as an automated claim transmitted once a month from a server outside every maintained route. A ward cooperative had filed intent to occupy the lower floors. Lake Junction had inspected the lobby but had neither repaired nor secured the structure.

"Who did you steal from?" Basma asked.

Neve pointed at the coil. "The wall."

"Walls do not own copper."

"Good."

The transit guard said, "The public grid does."

"It wasn't connected to the public grid."

"It was inside the district."

"The district wasn't carrying it down twenty-three floors."

Darius attended because his inspection mark had declared the tower uninhabitable. He had expected a technical question about safe entry. Instead, Basma asked whether his red mark gave the city a claim over everything behind it.

"It prohibits entry."

"That was not the question."

"If entry is prohibited, removal is prohibited."

Neve turned toward him. "Then danger owns everything."

The room grew quiet.

Outside the hearing, residents stripped abandoned buildings every day. They took pipe for water, glass for clinics, doors for heat, wire for light, stone for graves, and desks for schools. Calling all removal theft would criminalize the city's construction. Calling all removal salvage would allow armed crews to empty structures while displaced families walked toward them.

Basma wrote four questions on the old departure board.

Was the structure occupied?

Had a claimant maintained or secured it?

Had the material been marked for public recovery?

Did removal destroy a foreseeable use greater than the salvage value?

The former corporation answered none of them. The cooperative answered the last: removing upper-floor copper would not prevent lower-floor occupation. The heat authority offered to purchase half the coil for pump controls. Neve wanted payment for the descent and recovery.

"You entered a prohibited structure," Darius said.

"Then fine the entry."

"You could have died."

"Then pay dangerous work."

The court fined her one ration credit for unauthorized entry, recognized the copper as recovered material, paid her a salvage share, and assigned the remainder to the heat authority. It also ordered public recovery marks posted on inspected buildings.

Nobody considered the ruling elegant.

By the end of the week, three hundred people had applied to strip buildings they had entered without permission the day before.

The first Salvage Court met twice weekly after that. Its judges learned to ask not who had owned a ruin before the Exchange, but who maintained it now, who intended to use it, who risked entry, and what the city would lose when an object moved.

Ownership returned as a schedule of obligations before it returned as a deed.

The tower was supposed to answer the housing problem.

Its lower twenty floors had intact structure. The western stairwell could be sealed. Rain capture on the roof could supplement the municipal main. Old office ventilation offered vertical distribution if engineers replaced the fans and cleaned every duct. At projected density, the tower could house twelve hundred people.

At actual density, it would house two thousand and begin killing them by winter.

Darius had written that in his report. The housing council had asked him to inspect it again.

"A report is not improved by repetition," he told Councillor Basma Rahal when she met them on the sixth floor.

"The waiting list changed."

"Concrete did not."

"The north camp lost a boiler."

"That does not add stairs."

Basma wore a green ward scarf over a patched pressure coat. Before the Exchange, she had managed a chain pharmacy. Afterward she had learned procurement, triage, crowd control, mediation, and how to make a refusal sound like a schedule.

"How many floors can we open?" she asked.

"I have not finished."

"How many if you had?"

"Fewer than you need."

"That number I already have."

They continued upward.

Families had occupied the seventh floor before authorization. Sheets divided conference rooms. A cooking pipe vented through a shattered window packed around with clay. Shoes lined the hall so meltwater would not enter sleeping areas. Someone had painted apartment numbers over corporate room labels.

7A had once been STRATEGIC PARTNERSHIPS.

7B had once been HUMAN CAPITAL.

7C had no door and was marked QUIET BABY.

A woman stepped from 7A holding a ladle.

"Inspection?" she asked.

"Yes," Darius said.

"We already passed."

"You already moved in."

"The clerk gave us a placement."

Basma said, "Temporary winter placement."

"It's winter."

"The placement ends at thaw."

The woman glanced at a window filmed with ice. "Tell it."

Her name was Mariam Diop. Her placement record listed three children, an injured brother, and a work assignment in the transit kitchen. She had sealed the room's floor penetrations, installed a carbon monitor, and built a heat reflector from polished advertising panels. The space was cleaner than the council chamber.

Darius examined the cooking vent.

"Too close to the intake," he said.

"The intake is blocked."

"That makes it worse."

"It makes it not an intake."

Neve hid a smile.

Darius pointed at the pipe joint. "When the wind reverses, smoke comes back."

"Wind doesn't come through."

"It will when the ice moves."

"Then I'll fix it."

"With what?"

Mariam lifted the ladle. "You came to inspect my wall, not my inventory."

Her brother laughed from behind the sheet. His laugh became a cough and did not stop for several seconds.

Darius looked toward the sound.

"Old injury," Mariam said.

"Lungs?"

"Everything."

The settlement required respiratory screening for tower placement. Mariam's file contained none for her brother.

Basma read the omission at the same time he did.

"He arrived after registration," she said quietly.

"He arrived after the gate closed," Mariam corrected.

"He cannot stay in shared air without a screen."

"The clinic said nine days."

"Then he should be in observation housing."

"Observation housing is full."

The rules offered three correct answers that could not coexist.

Darius inspected the brother. Fever normal. Oxygen low but stable. Cough dry. No visible conductive filaments, no patterned rash, no smell of sweet metal associated with active wirelung. Iris would have rejected every conclusion he drew from that list without proper equipment.

"Get him a clinic priority," Darius told Basma.

"Against whom?"

"That is your job."

"No. That is the sentence people use when they want their decision to belong to me."

Mariam stood between them. "Can he stay tonight?"

Darius looked at the children behind the sheet. One was doing sums on the back of a frozen corporate mission statement. Another repaired a sock. The smallest slept beneath two coats.

"Tonight," he said. "No communal kitchen. Keep the vent closed until we fit a backdraft plate."

"And tomorrow?"

"Clinic."

"And if they say nine days?"

He did not answer.

On the twelfth floor, they found the wet wall.

It ran through an open-plan office where two hundred desks had been removed, leaving pale rectangles in the floor grime. The eastern wall glistened from baseboard to ceiling. Red-black strands traced the old data conduits. Some were cable insulation. Some flexed when Neve's lamp crossed them.

Darius raised his fist.

Everyone stopped.

He sampled the air. Elevated organics. Conductivity near the wall. Heat four degrees above the floor. No spores detected, although no field test could prove absence. He listened.

Behind the wall, water moved through a pipe.

Something clicked twice.

Then four times.

Then twice.

Neve whispered, "Search pattern."

"No."

"It heard us stop."

"Search pattern" did not mean Wetwire understood a search.

WCT crews had used the phrase when a site began repeating the cadence of entry: two radio checks, four tool strikes, two steps; a door opening after the same question; a relay answering the pause between names. Responders randomized routes, call signs, light, and tool sequence because repetition gave the colony another rhythm to join.

Training said: when the site begins matching the team, stop supplying the pattern.

"Pipes change pressure."

The clicking repeated: two, four, two.

Basma backed toward the stairwell.

Darius marked the floor red.

"Seal from eleven to thirteen," he said. "No entry. Pull every family below ten until we open the wall from outside."

"That's seven hundred people," Basma said.

"Then start now."

"We do not have beds."

"You have a wall carrying warm conductive growth."

"You said the scanner found no filaments."

"The scanner samples the air."

"You don't know what it is."

"Correct."

"Then why are you evacuating three floors?"

Darius looked at her. "Because I don't know what it is."

The order traveled faster than its explanation.

By dusk, the western stairwell filled with families carrying blankets, food tins, tools, documents, icons, water filters, and the particular useless objects people preserved when told they had ten minutes. A boy carried the plastic shell of a game console. An old woman carried a ceramic bowl without food in it. Mariam brought the ladle.

The transit platform became emergency housing. Trains had not moved there in fourteen years, but the departure board still flickered when power demand dropped. Families slept beneath destinations no corridor reached.

ANGER SPREAD WITH THE COLD.

Someone painted that on the inspection office.

Darius pretended not to admire the accuracy.

The exterior team opened the twelfth-floor wall the next morning from a suspended maintenance cage. They wore sealed suits and carried Vulcanite lances left from WCT stores. Residents gathered beyond the exclusion line despite instructions. Children climbed ventilation housings for a better view until Neve drove them down.

Darius entered through the cut.

Inside the wall, an old fire-suppression pipe had split. Water flowed across bundled network cable and into a cavity lined with insulation. The red-black strands were fungal roots feeding on cellulose backing and metal-rich dust. The heat came from an emergency battery pack buried beneath debris.

The clicking came from a relay.

When water bridged two contacts, it repeated an evacuation code.

There was no active Wetwire.

Neve looked through the opening. "Good news."

Darius stared at the fungal mat.

"Say it," she told him.

"We were wrong."

"You were wrong."

"The inspection unit was wrong."

"You gave the order."

He turned off the relay. The clicking stopped.

No one died because he had evacuated the floors. That fact would support him at the hearing. No one could prove the evacuation had been unnecessary until after it happened. That fact would support him too.

Seven hundred people had spent a night on a platform. Two elderly residents developed hypothermia. A communal kitchen lost half its food when an unattended heating pipe burst. Mariam's brother missed the clinic appointment Darius had demanded. The next available screening was eleven days away.

The hearing filled the old ticket hall.

Darius described the sensor readings, visual evidence, unknown growth, and history of infrastructure infestation. He did not say that Neve's phrase, *search pattern*, had changed his breathing. He did not say he had remembered office buildings whose vents pulsed after power was cut. He did not say five years had passed without a confirmed active Wetwire site inside Lake Junction and that part of him distrusted the absence.

Mariam spoke after him.

"He saw a wall he did not understand," she said. "So we lost our rooms."

"He protected the tower," a delegate replied.

"From a battery."

"From an unknown hazard."

"Everything is an unknown hazard to somebody with authority."

The room shifted at that.

Darius expected Basma to defend the evacuation. Instead she placed the housing records on the table.

"The residents were in the tower because my council assigned them before inspection was complete," she said. "We converted a technical uncertainty into an emergency because we had already converted emergency housing into permanent housing without admitting it."

The council did not remove Darius. It required two inspectors to approve future three-floor evacuations unless air tests confirmed active contamination. It also ordered the tower reopened through the eleventh floor, funded a second stair enclosure, and recognized residents already placed there as tenants rather than temporary occupants.

Every side claimed partial victory.

Mariam returned to 7A.

Darius fitted the backdraft plate himself.

Her brother watched from a chair near the window.

"Clinic?" Darius asked.

"Nine days," Mariam said.

"I can request priority."

"Against whom?"

She used Basma's exact words.

Darius tightened the last fastener. "No one speaks like themselves anymore."

"Maybe you all finally listen."

He tested the vent. The plate opened outward and sealed when the pressure reversed.

Mariam set a bowl of stew on the table. "You eating?"

"Is that forgiveness?"

"It's lentils."

"We don't have lentils."

"Then don't inspect them."

He ate standing beside the wall.

At the end of the month, the council declared the first eleven floors habitable. Families painted over corporate lettering but left some of the old words visible. HUMAN CAPITAL became a nursery. CONTINUITY PLANNING became a laundry. FUTURE SYSTEMS housed a family who repaired hand pumps.

The tower did not become safe.

It became inhabited.

That distinction was written into the occupancy certificate at Neve's insistence.

Below it, in smaller print, the council added:

*Habitation requires continuous inspection, maintenance, and the right to challenge closure.*

Darius signed the certificate.

Mariam signed beneath him as resident representative.

Neither signature made the building belong to them.

It made their disagreement part of the structure.

## Chapter 2 - Refuge-Polities

### 2085

Oksana Mirek became governor because the carbon scrubbers recognized her thumb.

The title came later. At first she was simply the last engineer awake when the refuge sealed.

Morrow Refuge had begun as a private research campus beneath a hill outside the northern industrial belt. Its owners expected storms, sabotage, civil unrest, and brief periods of biological quarantine. They did not expect the Exchange. The underground complex had laboratories, machine shops, a clinic, dormitories, hydroponic rooms, a deep well, and enough hardened generation to keep four hundred specialists alive for eighteen months.

Seven years later, 1,640 people lived inside it.

The specialists had become residents. Residents had become families. Families had taken over laboratories after discovering that children could not sleep beside centrifuges forever. Every corridor held pipes added after construction. Every room served at least two purposes. The materials lab became a dining hall at noon and a classroom after dishes were stacked. The simulation theater held public meetings until a ceiling projector failed and began displaying weather models from 2076 across every debate.

On the morning the heat formula went into effect, an extinct hurricane rotated behind Oksana's head.

"Contribution units are not wages," she told the assembly.

A man near the rear called, "Then why do they decide where I sleep?"

"They determine priority during thermal load."

"That's wages with a blanket."

The room laughed. Oksana waited.

She had learned that speaking over people made them louder and waiting made them suspect the air system had failed.

"The lower dormitories hold heat," she said. "The upper labs lose it. We cannot place everyone below. The formula gives priority to residents whose work sustains refuge systems, residents with medical vulnerability, and children under five."

"And everybody else?"

"Rotates."

"My mother is sixty-eight."

"Her clinic assessment assigns vulnerability."

"Clinic says age isn't a diagnosis."

"Correct."

"She can't climb six levels twice a week."

"Mobility support is a separate allocation."

This time no one laughed.

Oksana had written the formula with two systems engineers, a nurse, a nutrition planner, and a resident selected by lottery. It measured work hours, critical skill coverage, medical risk, dependent children, room heat loss, and distance from toilets. They had tested it against six months of housing records. It reduced favoritism, protected the clinic, and prevented the machine-shop crews from claiming every warm room by threatening to stop repairs.

The formula also assigned Mari Alvarez to Upper Lab Four.

Mari stood in the aisle with a sleeping child tied against her chest and another holding her coat.

"I clean the surgical room," she said.

"Four hours per day," Oksana replied.

"Four there. Three in recovery. Two washing linens when the pump works."

"The work log shows four."

"Because recovery doesn't have a supervisor."

"Then the log is incomplete."

"The floor isn't."

Mari's voice had the quiet edge of a person too tired to spend volume. Her oldest child, Jo, stared at Oksana without blinking. The child on her chest moved once and settled.

"Submit a correction," Oksana said.

"To housing?"

"Work allocation."

"They sent me to housing."

"The correction begins with your shift coordinator."

"He died in October."

Oksana looked toward the work-allocation table. The clerk examined a screen and nodded.

"Who approves her hours now?" Oksana asked.

"Environmental medicine absorbed the unit," he said.

"Then environmental medicine."

"Their coordinator is in isolation."

"Deputy."

"Vacant."

Mari said, "Should I ask the floor?"

The extinct hurricane continued turning.

Oksana could override the assignment. The old emergency system allowed it because the old emergency system allowed her to override nearly everything. She had used that authority to prevent food technicians from moving their relatives ahead of a refugee intake, to isolate a generator crew after a wirelung exposure, and to open private executive stores when the owners ordered her to wait for legal approval from a government no longer transmitting.

Each use had been necessary.

Each necessity remained in the access system.

"You will remain in Lower Dormitory Two for seventy-two hours," Oksana said. "Work allocation will reconstruct your hours."

Mari shifted the infant's weight. "And after?"

"The formula will recalculate."

"You made the formula."

"Six people made it."

"Does it know that?"

The question followed Oksana out of the assembly.

Her workroom had once controlled environmental testing chambers. Three walls were transparent, though residents had covered two with insulation panels. On the exposed wall, technicians could watch her work, eat, and occasionally sleep beside the control desk. The refuge called that transparency.

Her deputy, Anton Mbeki, called it punishment.

"You could hang a blanket," he said.

"Then they will say I hide decisions."

"You do hide decisions."

"I publish every allocation."

"You decide which allocations exist."

Anton set two cups on the desk. One contained chicory brewed from a recovery seed batch. The other contained hot water because the kitchen had exhausted its daily chicory allocation.

Oksana took the water.

"How many appeals?" she asked.

"One hundred and twelve."

"The model predicted thirty."

"The model assumed complete work logs."

"We sampled them."

"From technical units."

"They were the units with records."

"That is why the records were complete."

Anton had repaired laboratory robotics before the Exchange. He now supervised housing because he could understand dependency chains and because everyone else who understood them had refused. He spoke slowly, leaving room for disagreement, which made his criticism harder to dismiss.

He opened the appeal summary.

Caregivers were undercounted. Informal teaching was absent. Food preparation inside households did not count unless performed in the common kitchen. Residents who repaired clothing, watched children during shifts, cleaned unassigned corridors, or cared for injured relatives appeared less useful than residents whose work passed through a terminal.

"We included dependents," Oksana said.

"We counted the burden. Not the work."

"A formula cannot evaluate every private activity."

"Then it cannot decide every room."

"What do you propose?"

"Ward allocation."

"The machine-shop ward will keep the lower level."

"They elected two caregivers last month."

"They elected the shop chief's sister."

"Who runs the nursery."

"Because the shop reassigned her there after she lost two fingers."

"You hear corruption. They hear someone they know."

Oksana drank the hot water. It tasted faintly of metal, although the morning report said metal content was normal.

"The lower dormitories are a physical system," she said. "Heat capacity, occupancy, airflow, infection load. Familiarity does not alter thermodynamics."

"No. It alters whether people obey."

The word bothered her.

"Residents comply because the constraints are real."

"Residents comply because you control the doors."

Morrow's original access system divided the refuge into clean, restricted, hazardous, and executive zones. The executive category had been removed during the first winter. The others remained. Oksana's thumb could open all of them. Most residents' credentials opened the route between assigned housing and assigned work.

The restrictions limited contamination and theft. They also meant a mother whose shift record was wrong could be locked away from the room where her children slept.

"Bring me alternatives by evening," Oksana said.

"I just did."

"Alternatives that work."

Anton left his untouched chicory on the desk.

At midday, the west scrubber began drawing excess current.

The alarm sounded first in the environmental room and three seconds later in every occupied corridor. Conversations stopped. Children moved toward wall marks before adults instructed them. Kitchen staff sealed food. Clinic staff closed internal doors. Seven years of drills converted alarm into choreography.

Oksana reached the scrubber gallery in ninety seconds.

Two technicians had opened the diagnostics. Pressure remained stable. Carbon dioxide climbed slowly in the west habitation loop. The drive motor pulled twelve percent above expected load.

"Bearing?" she asked.

"Temperature normal," said Asha Pell, the shift lead.

"Filter resistance?"

"High, but not this high."

"Bypass west through central."

"Central is carrying Upper Three."

"Evacuate Upper Three."

Asha looked at her.

"Housing rotation put three hundred people there this morning."

"Then central cannot carry both."

"If we evacuate now, the doors send them to lower assembly."

Lower assembly was already occupied by residents appealing the heat formula.

Oksana entered her override and opened the bypass.

The motor pitch changed. Pressure recovered. Carbon dioxide leveled.

"Pull the filter bank," she said.

Four technicians released the first cassette. It moved ten centimeters and stopped.

Something had grown through it.

The material was pale gray, fibrous, and dry. It threaded through filter media and around the metal frame. Asha sprayed it with diagnostic reagent. No Wetwire reaction. No known fungal fluorescence. The growth did not move.

"Kill the loop," Oksana said.

"West habitation loses ventilation."

"Masks and corridor transfer."

"Lower assembly?"

"Open the dining hall."

"Food service is active."

"Close it."

Every instruction solved one system by displacing another.

By afternoon, the refuge ate cold food in housing corridors while technicians dismantled the west scrubber. Residents from Upper Three carried bedding down stairwells and discovered that the lower assembly had no room for them. Appeals clerks moved to the machine shop. The machine shop objected because open records introduced paper dust near precision surfaces.

Mari Alvarez arrived at the scrubber gallery wearing cleaning coveralls.

"Restricted area," Oksana said.

"Environmental medicine sent me."

"For what?"

"Decontamination."

"We have technicians."

"They clean machines. I clean what comes out."

Mari inspected the removed filter cassette without touching it.

"This isn't dry," she said.

"Humidity sensor reads twelve percent."

"Sensor is upstream."

She pointed beneath the frame. A thin line of condensation ran from a cracked cleaning nozzle into the filter housing. The gray fibers followed it.

"We flushed the bank last month," Asha said.

"You flushed around the bank," Mari replied. "Drain backs up."

"The drain report was clear."

"Who cleaned it?"

"Automated cycle."

Mari looked at Oksana. "Does the formula give the cycle a warm room?"

Asha choked on a laugh and turned it into a cough.

The growth proved to be an ordinary fungal colony feeding on trapped dust and cleaning compounds. It had not threatened the refuge until it obstructed the scrubber. The repair took eighteen hours. Mari showed the technicians where the drain channel narrowed behind a casing no schematic included.

"How did you know?" Oksana asked.

"It smells like the recovery ward."

Oksana smelled hot metal, old filters, sealant, and human sweat.

"I don't smell it."

"That's why you have meters."

The refuge restored west ventilation before dawn.

Oksana did not sleep. She sat in the transparent workroom with the housing formula open on one screen and the scrubber maintenance logs on another. Both systems shared the same defect. They recorded assigned work. They ignored the labor that kept assigned work possible.

At 06:00, she disabled the formula.

By 06:04, the machine-shop chief demanded to know why his crew's lower-room priority had vanished. At 06:11, the clinic warned that ward allocation might place respiratory patients near kitchens. At 06:20, a ward delegate tried to reserve a dormitory before the replacement rules existed.

Anton arrived with chicory for both of them.

"That was quick," he said.

"They were waiting."

"People often are."

"Ward allocation will reproduce patronage."

"Probably."

"The formula reproduced records."

"Also patronage. Better dressed."

Oksana opened a blank policy.

"Hybrid system," she said. "Environmental limits set capacity. Wards assign rooms within them. Appeals are public. Care work receives logged equivalence."

"Who logs private care?"

"Households attest."

"They will lie."

"Supervisors lied."

"Correct."

"You sound pleased."

"I like when a problem becomes ordinary."

She wrote until the assembly filled again.

Mari stood near the front with both children awake this time. Jo held a paper model of the west scrubber made from folded ration notices.

Oksana explained the new system without calling it improved.

"It will fail differently," she said. "We will review it in thirty days."

A man asked, "Who can override it?"

Oksana looked at the access panel beside the stage.

"I can."

"Still?"

"The scrubber system requires an emergency controller."

"Do the bedrooms?"

No engineering answer could separate them while the same doors controlled both.

"Not permanently," she said.

"When does permanent start?"

Anton folded his arms.

Oksana could have said after winter, after the next filter shipment, after the population fell below design load, after external government returned. The refuge had used every answer before.

"It started before we admitted it," she said.

Silence moved through the room.

"Then we end it in pieces. Housing overrides transfer to a five-person ward review. Contamination seals remain with environmental control. Every use is published by shift end. My universal credential is copied to two elected custodians and one clinic officer."

The work-allocation clerk raised his hand. "That increases security risk."

"Yes."

"You agree?"

"I heard you."

The assembly approved the transfer by a margin smaller than Oksana expected.

Some residents trusted her more than elected ward delegates. Some benefited from the technical hierarchy. Some feared that five people would respond more slowly than one when the air failed. Survival authority had not persisted only because engineers refused to release it. Residents had built their routines around knowing whom to blame.

After the meeting, Jo approached Oksana with the paper scrubber.

"Mama says this is your machine."

"Your mother understands it better."

"But you tell it what to do."

"Sometimes."

"Does it listen?"

"Usually."

Jo handed her the model. On one side, in a child's block letters, she had written AIR. On the other she had written PEOPLE.

"Which side goes in?" Oksana asked.

Jo frowned at the obvious stupidity of the question.

"Both."

Oksana placed the model above her control desk.

That evening, technicians replaced her universal thumb authorization with four separate credentials. The process took eleven minutes. Nothing failed. No doors locked. No scrubber stopped.

Oksana had expected relief.

What she felt was the unease of a person who had mistaken being necessary for being ready.

Outside her transparent wall, Mari cleaned the assembly floor after the vote. Her work now appeared in the refuge ledger.

The hours were wrong.

Mari corrected them herself.

That winter, two smaller settlements merged their registries at the Sayegh Archive and discovered they had married the same couple differently.

The north intake settlement recognized Hana and Sorel because they had shared a ration account, named each other during a fever quarantine, and occupied one room for three consecutive winters. The tram-cut settlement recognized them because twelve witnesses remembered a ceremony beside a disabled train.

The trouble was that each registry claimed its own recognition had come first.

That mattered for inheritance. Hana's brother had died between the two possible dates, leaving a tool chest, a respirator, and a disputed half share in a water cart. Under the north intake rule, the tools had entered the marriage household. Under the tram-cut rule, they had passed first to Hana alone. Under the Archive's old civil code, neither union existed because no surviving state officer had licensed it.

The merger clerk brought all three answers to Nadiya.

"Which ceremony was first?" Nadiya asked.

Hana and Sorel looked at each other.

"The train," Sorel said.

"The quarantine," Hana said at the same time.

People in the registry queue laughed. Hana did not.

"The train ceremony was when everyone came," she said. "But we had already been together."

"We had not said vows."

"You promised to clean the mask filters."

"That was not a vow. That was hygiene."

"You still failed."

The laughter returned, including Sorel's this time.

Nadiya asked for the train witnesses. Seven were alive. Two remembered the ceremony before the quarantine. Three remembered it after. One remembered only the soup. The last insisted there had been no ceremony, merely a meal at which Hana's mother had embarrassed everyone.

The north intake registry produced ration sheets bearing both names. The first had no date because its corner had been burned. A later sheet identified them as one household. The quarantine roster named each as the other's medical decision contact.

"Choose the earliest provable date," the merger clerk said.

"Provable by which settlement's proof?" Nadiya asked.

"That is why we brought it to the Archive."

"You brought it here so our uncertainty could outrank yours."

The clerk lowered his voice. "If we do not choose, every inheritance entry after theirs remains open."

Behind him, the queue extended into the corridor. People waited to merge births, deaths, work obligations, guardianships, and names spelled differently by exhausted record keepers. Two settlements were becoming one polity through fields on a form.

Nadiya turned back to the couple.

"When did you begin treating the other as your spouse?"

Hana said, "Before the train."

Sorel said, "During the first cold month."

"Did either of you stop?"

"Many times," Hana said.

Sorel frowned. "We argued."

"Did you withdraw the relationship?"

"No."

"Did you tell others it had ended?"

"No."

"Did you continue sharing work, shelter, care, and public responsibility?"

Hana looked at the repaired sleeve on Sorel's coat. Sorel looked at the respirator hanging from Hana's chair.

"Yes," they said.

Nadiya pulled the three incompatible records together. She did not select a ceremony date. Instead she wrote:

*Marriage recognized by continuous mutual acknowledgment.*

The merger clerk read it twice.

"What date do I enter?"

"The earliest date both acknowledge the continuity."

"That is not the ceremony."

"No."

"The witnesses will object."

"They may record the ceremonies too."

Hana leaned over the table. "Does this mean I own the tool chest?"

"It means the marriage household's claim began before your brother died, if Sorel confirms the same date."

Sorel examined the tools listed in the inheritance file.

"She maintained them."

"That is a different claim."

"It is the true one."

Nadiya added a maintenance interest beside the marriage interest and sent the tool chest to allocation review. The couple left legally married and still uncertain who owned the respirator wrench.

The sentence remained in the registry.

Other couples used it: people whose witnesses had died, people who had exchanged vows in languages no merger clerk spoke, people whose old governments prohibited their marriage, people who had never held a ceremony because every available room had been needed for sleep.

Continuous mutual acknowledgment did not end disputes. It moved the proof from a vanished authority into repeated conduct between people.

A doctrine began because Nadiya refused to decide whose ceremony had been real.

## Chapter 3 - First Caravans

### 2087

Talia Sen knew six ways to tell whether a caravan had stopped for danger.

Adults stopped talking.

Drivers left engines running.

Scouts looked backward before they looked ahead.

The cook covered the fire without putting it out.

Someone checked the children and pretended not to.

Rami Vale sharpened the blue pencil.

He sat on the lead carrier's rear step with a paper map across his knees. The map had been folded so often that its original roads met along white tears. Blue lines crossed them, doubled back, ended at warnings, and continued in different hands. Rami used a small knife to expose more pencil lead.

Talia climbed down from the cargo bed.

"Why are we stopped?"

"Bridge."

"Broken?"

"Present."

"That's good."

"Not always."

Rami was seventeen and spoke to children as if they had asked technical questions. This was why Talia preferred him to adults, who heard fear whenever she wanted information.

The caravan occupied a dead highway between two walls of wind-packed snow. Twenty-three vehicles had left Lake Junction nine days earlier: electric freight haulers, two tracked carriers, a medical van, a water tender, and three old combustion trucks reserved for cold starts. Their cargo included antibiotics, machined pump seals, seed cultures descended from Sade Adebayo's public releases, repaired respirator valves, glass ampoules, paper, salt, and news.

News traveled in a locked case because people paid more for it when it appeared official.

Talia's mother, Meera Sen, called this stupid and charged for it anyway.

Ahead, the highway crossed a river through a suspension bridge that had survived the Exchange, spring floods, two unrecorded battles, and five years without inspection. Rami's map marked it passable in 2085. A red thread tied around the page indicated disputed information.

An old woman waited beside the approach in a yellow coat.

She had no vehicle, pack, or visible weapon. Snow reached her knees.

The scouts spoke to her through mask amplifiers. Their voices carried back in fragments.

"...not the deck..."

"...heard it last night..."

"...south service..."

Rami folded the map.

"What did she say?" Talia asked.

"River moved under the west pier. Bridge makes noise."

"Bridges make noise."

"She says this one says names."

Talia waited for him to laugh.

He did not.

Hollow steel could imitate voices in wind. Wetwire nests could store routine sounds. People near Echo Bloom fields on a world not yet known to humanity would one day build ghost stories from replayed voices, but on Earth in 2087 there were enough mechanisms for haunting without inventing spirits.

Meera came forward from the trade carrier. She was short, broad-shouldered, and wore three currencies inside her coat because no gate trusted the same promises.

"Local?" she asked Rami.

"No settlement marker."

"Did you ask her?"

"Scouts did."

"Scouts ask whether people have guns. Traders ask where they sleep."

Meera approached the woman herself.

They spoke for several minutes. The old woman pointed downstream, then toward a service track disappearing beneath snow. Meera returned with a strip of dried apple.

"Her name is Olya," she said. "She lives at the bridge station with eleven people. Two are under the deck repairing cable anchors."

"The bridge is maintained?" Rami asked.

"The bridge is inhabited."

"Why isn't it marked?"

"Because you haven't been here."

He ignored that. "What says names?"

"Expansion joints. Wind comes from the service tunnel and makes syllables."

"Which names?"

"Whichever ones people bring."

The caravan master, Javed Cole, gathered the route council beside the lead carrier. He was Darius Cole's nephew and had inherited neither Darius's patience for meetings nor his belief that every dirty job could be solved with the correct bucket.

"Map says bridge," Javed said.

Meera pointed downstream. "Locals say service ford."

"In this temperature?"

"Ice over old maintenance road."

"Weight?"

"They move cable carts."

"Cable carts are not freight haulers."

Rami laid the map on the carrier hood. "The bridge carried three caravans in the last two years."

Olya spoke from behind them.

"It carried two."

Rami turned. "The Iron Church convoy crossed in March."

"They came back."

"That is still a crossing."

"Not to where they wanted."

The wind moved through the bridge. Metal groaned. For a moment, Talia heard a long vowel rise from the cables.

It sounded like her father's name because she had been waiting three years to hear it.

She looked at Meera.

Her mother was watching the bridge too.

Javed sent Neve Okonkwo's inspection pair onto the deck with tether lines and ultrasonic hammers. The caravan waited. Engines consumed charge. Children became hungry. Drivers began arguing about whether delay posed more risk than the crossing.

Rami studied the old survey.

"West pier readings were good," he said.

Meera crouched beside Talia to retie her boot. "Good when?"

"Two years."

"Before two thaws."

"The service road isn't mapped."

"The people using it are."

"People lie."

"Maps don't?"

"Maps show when they're wrong."

"Only after somebody pays."

Rami's hand closed around the pencil.

Talia had seen adults find the place inside him where the missing became a route problem. His grandmother had been assigned to a western shelter during the Exchange. He had marked every signal, caravan account, burial list, and camp register that might contain her. Each contradiction meant someone had drawn the world badly. Better lines could still return her.

The inspection pair came back.

Neve removed her mask. Frost marked the seal around her face.

"Deck is sound through the first span," she said. "West suspender load is uneven. Can't inspect the pier without going below."

"Can we cross?" Javed asked.

"Light vehicles, one at a time. No stops."

"Freight?"

"I said light."

"If we redistribute?"

"Then you have many light vehicles and cargo on this side."

The medical van carried temperature-sensitive antibiotics requested by the Sayegh Archive and two smaller settlements. Its battery could not support a day-long detour. The freight haulers carried pump seals needed at a flooded refuge. The water tender was nearly empty and could cross light, but its storage bladders were part of the trade.

Javed looked at the sky. Snow would begin within two hours.

"We cross," he said.

Meera said, "We use the service road."

"Unrated."

"Maintained."

"By twelve people."

"That's twelve more than the bridge."

Rami said, "Every delay risks the medicine."

"Every crossing risks the van," Meera replied.

"The bridge carried military loads."

"The military is gone."

"The steel isn't."

Olya watched them argue as if this were another form of weather.

Talia pulled the red thread from the map.

Rami noticed. "Put that back."

"What does it mean?"

"Disputed."

"You're not disputing."

He took the thread from her and tied it around the bridge again.

Javed ordered the two scout vehicles across. Both reached the far side. Then the water tender. The bridge groaned but held.

The medical van went fourth.

Halfway across the second span, its rear axle dropped.

The deck did not collapse. A maintenance plate beneath the left wheel folded into an access cavity, trapping the tire between steel ribs. The van tilted. Inside, the medicine refrigerator alarm began.

No one spoke.

The sound from the bridge changed as the van's weight pulled the span.

Wind passed through the service tunnel.

Talia heard her father's name again.

Rami ran onto the deck before Neve caught his coat.

"Tether."

"The fridge battery."

"Tether."

They reached the van with jacks, cable, and a portable spreader. The driver crawled out through the passenger side. Neve inspected the ribs while Rami transferred medicine cases to the scout vehicle on the far side.

The western suspender moved twelve millimeters during the first lift.

"Stop," Neve said.

"We have it," Rami replied.

"The bridge has us."

Javed ordered everyone back. Rami refused until he had the last antibiotic case.

They abandoned the van.

At the near approach, Meera slapped Javed hard enough to turn his face.

Nobody moved to stop her.

"That for the vehicle?" he asked.

"That was for deciding before the council finished."

"Medicine crossed."

"Because people pulled it."

"It crossed."

"Say that to the driver whose home is hanging over the river."

The van had been his home for four years. Photographs, spare clothes, letters, cooking tin, and his wife's ashes remained inside.

The caravan spent the afternoon lowering cargo by rope to the service road. Olya's settlement brought sledges and two cable carts. Children carried light packages. Drivers cursed every kilometer of the detour. The abandoned van remained visible above them until snow erased it.

At the bridge station, Olya's people served hot water flavored with apple peel. Their home occupied old maintenance rooms built into the abutment. Hammocks hung above cable spools. A shrine held bolts, photographs, a wooden cross, a brass prayer plate, and the names of three people written on the wall.

One name belonged to Talia's father.

Meera sat down without removing her pack.

"Where did you get that?" she asked.

Olya pointed to a man repairing a winch.

He had found Arun Sen two winters earlier in a failed shelter west of the river. Arun had traveled with six people. Two survived long enough to reach the bridge station. He did not.

Olya brought Meera a sealed metal box.

Inside were Arun's identity card, a broken watch, two letters, and a route notebook.

Talia had imagined her father returning so many times that the dead object of him felt like another wrong answer.

"Did he say anything?" she asked.

Olya considered.

"He complained about the soup."

Meera laughed once, covered her mouth, and bent over the box.

"That's him," she said.

No final speech. No message for a child he had not expected to miss. He had complained about soup and recorded three contaminated wells in a notebook.

Rami sat outside the station with the map.

Talia joined him after dark.

He had crossed out the bridge in blue. Beneath it he drew the service road from Olya's directions.

"You were wrong," Talia said.

"Yes."

He did not defend himself. That made her angrier.

"You almost lost the medicine."

"We didn't."

"You almost lost Neve."

"She went because Javed ordered the crossing."

"You told him the map was good."

"I told him what it said."

"You wanted it to be right."

Rami looked toward the bridge. Its cables sounded above the river.

"If the map can be wrong after two years, then every route to my grandmother can be wrong."

"She might be dead."

"Your father was."

Talia regretted the sentence before it ended.

Rami folded the map carefully along its oldest crease.

"Yes," he said.

She sat beside him.

After a while, he handed her the blue pencil.

"Mark your father."

"He's not a place."

"He was here."

Talia drew a small square beside the bridge station. She wrote ARUN SEN and then, because the letters looked too final, added COMPLAINED ABOUT SOUP.

Rami did not correct it.

The caravan reached the Sayegh Archive twelve days after leaving Lake Junction.

The antibiotics remained cold. One pump-seal crate had cracked. Three seed cultures spoiled. The news case arrived locked and contained reports already known at the archive through radio. Meera sold them anyway because paper could be carried into rooms where radios could not.

Nadiya Sayegh received the route map at a table that had once held rescue votes.

She was older than Talia expected. Her hair was white. Two fingers on her left hand no longer straightened. She examined the blue correction at the bridge and the square bearing Arun's name.

"This is not a route notation," she said.

Talia held her breath.

Nadiya added the map to the atlas.

"It is still information."

Rami asked for the latest western shelter lists. Nadiya brought him three. None contained his grandmother.

That evening, archive residents and caravan crews ate together beside the warm trunk. People traded rumors across bowls. A mechanic claimed Lake Junction's tower had begun producing its own weather. A child insisted Morrow Refuge's doors could recognize liars. Someone from the archive said the Yamama wells reached the center of Earth. Meera sold a bridge warning to two departing traders and gave it free to a family traveling on foot.

Talia listened to strangers complain about salt, sleeping space, gate fees, and whose turn it was to wash bowls.

For the first time in her life, another settlement's ordinary problems felt reachable.

Before the caravan left, Nadiya returned the route map in a waxed sleeve.

"The archive keeps a copy," she said.

Rami inspected the copy mark. "Which is official?"

"The one on the road."

"Paper isn't a road."

"No," Nadiya said. "People are."

Rami tied the blue pencil around his neck.

Talia carried her father's notebook.

The caravan moved east with medicine exchanged for filters, filters exchanged for salt contracts, salt contracts exchanged for promises that might survive long enough to become trade.

Behind them, the archive answered the morning.

Ahead, the bridge continued saying names.

## Chapter 4 - Drift Toward City-States

### 2090

The gate clerk asked Hessa Markovic where she lived.

Hessa looked back at the road.

Her house lay eighty-three kilometers west, beside a reservoir whose pumps had failed in spring. Her husband and eldest daughter were buried behind it. The kitchen still contained six jars of beans, a cracked stove, and the height marks of three children on the doorframe. Snow had taken part of the roof. Mold had taken the rest.

She had lived there.

She did not live anywhere now.

"Last fixed residence," the clerk said.

"Kamen Reservoir."

The clerk searched the registry. "Unrecognized site."

"It was there."

"I mean no active civil code."

"We had rules."

"Which authority issued your departure paper?"

Hessa held up the paper. It bore the reservoir council's stamp, two signatures, and a grease mark from the vehicle that had carried them east.

"The council."

"The registry does not have its identity key."

"The identity key left before we did."

Behind her, the entry line extended through a covered concrete lane. Families stood between freight carts and quarantine barriers. Gate drones moved above them on ceiling tracks, sampling air, clothing, baggage, and the mud beneath boots. Every ten minutes, a recorded voice instructed entrants not to conceal respiratory symptoms.

Talia Sen worked three desks away as a corridor witness.

At nineteen, she had learned that most borders were furniture arranged around a queue. The Lake Junction entry hall used folding tables from a school, barriers from a transit station, scanners from a hospital, and law copied from the Sayegh Archive's registry. None of it had been designed to make a city. Together it decided who could enter one.

The clerk turned Hessa's paper over.

"Household members?"

"Four living."

"Four present?"

"Three present. My son went south with the pump crew."

"Then he requires a separate claim."

"He's twelve."

"If he arrives with the crew, they register him."

"With me."

"He is not here."

Hessa put both hands on the table. "Put him with me."

The clerk looked toward Talia.

Corridor witnesses existed because registries did not trust unverified travelers and travelers did not trust registries. A witness could attach route history, settlement testimony, trade records, and known family connections to an entry claim. It did not guarantee admission. It made refusal harder to automate.

Talia joined them.

"Name?" she asked.

"Hessa Markovic."

"His."

"Petar. Petar Markovic. Twelve years old. Burn scar on his right wrist. He lies about his age when work crews count portions."

"Who led the pump crew?"

"Deni Kovac."

Talia knew the name. Deni traded electrical winding and bad tobacco along the western reservoir route.

"I can attest the crew exists," she told the clerk.

"Can you attest the child joined it?"

"No."

"Then I cannot attach him to the household."

Hessa said, "You can. You won't."

The clerk's face tightened. "If I add an absent dependent without verification, someone else can arrive under his name."

"Then ask if he's got the scar."

"Scars can be copied."

"Not by him. He cries if you clean it."

The clerk lowered her voice. "This rule keeps children from being claimed by strangers."

"I'm his mother."

"That is the claim."

Hessa began to cry without changing expression.

Talia took the file to Dr. Iris Bell-Reyes.

Iris worked behind the medical review partition with a magnifying lens clipped over one eye. At sixty, she moved slowly until a patient needed something. Then everyone else discovered they had been wasting motion.

"Kamen Reservoir," Talia said.

"Failed pump."

"You know it?"

"We received their water-borne illness totals."

"They had no identity key."

"Disease reports require fewer proofs than people."

Iris read Hessa's departure paper.

"Add the absent-child field," Talia said.

"It exists."

"The clerk can't attach him."

"Correct."

"Change the rule."

"No."

Talia waited for an explanation.

Iris did not provide one.

"He's twelve."

"According to a woman we have not screened."

"I believe her."

"Then you have confused your work with the system's."

"My work is witnessing."

"Your work is recording what you witnessed."

"What's the difference?"

"Power."

Iris removed the magnifying lens.

"Before the Exchange, civil databases separated families because forms did not admit how families existed. Afterward, we left room. Then people sold rooms. They claimed dead children for ration priority. They claimed living children for labor custody. They built families out of names recovered from burial lists."

"So we treat everyone like a trafficker."

"No. We record uncertainty."

"The gate treats uncertainty like refusal."

Iris looked toward the entry hall. "That is not what I wrote."

"It is what you built."

The sentence landed between them.

Iris had created parent fields with blank lines, linked records without ownership language, and preserved missing people as unresolved rather than dead. Those practices had reunited households. They had also given cities a detailed instrument for deciding which relationships counted.

"Bring the mother in," Iris said.

Hessa answered questions for forty minutes.

Petar hated cooked carrots, slept with his hands beneath his face, and had broken his wrist at six after climbing a spillway ladder. His father set the bone because the reservoir clinic was closed. Petar's sister taught him to read pump labels. He joined Deni's crew because the family's departure vehicle had room for three.

Iris entered each statement as unverified testimony.

"This does not prove parentage," she said.

"I know my son."

"I believe you do."

"Then why write unverified?"
