Labor councils demanded ordinary representation. Restoration programs wanted specialists who could remain accountable across projects lasting centuries. City governments wanted an Immortal associated with their bloc while denying that prestige affected selection.

Noa Bell-Reyes applied.

So did Anika Mirek, Samira al-Khatib, Farid Volkov-Rao, Yasmin Sayegh, Amari Adebayo, Tomas Keene-Cole, Nkiru Okafor-Bhandari, Lucien Moreau, Asha Said, Mateo Quispe Alvarez, and Keiko Tanaka.

Elian read the list.

"Twelve," Ilyana said.

"Thirteen with me."

"You sound pleased."

"Terrified."

"Good."

She had begun to gray completely. He had not changed enough for either of them to prove the treatment without a laboratory.

Ilyana touched his face.

"When they make statues," she said, "I'm telling everyone you snore."

"Who is making statues?"

She looked toward the clinic entrance, where strangers had begun leaving flowers.

"Your family," she said. "Eventually."

## Chapter 11 - The Remaining Twelve

### 2205-2220

The thirteenth chair became a constitutional problem before anyone sat in it.

The Great Cities supplied twelve seals, twelve voting delegations, and twelve claims that their candidate represented a distinct part of Earth. Elian Voss represented Morrow Refuge already, which meant Morrow would have two Immortals while every other city had one.

Morrow's council called this coincidence.

Lake Junction called it capture.

The Immortals called it irrelevant.

No one believed them.

Stabilization proceeded over five years.

Farid Volkov-Rao and Noa Bell-Reyes completed treatment in 2205. Samira al-Khatib and Nkiru Okafor-Bhandari followed in 2206. Anika Mirek and Mateo Quispe Alvarez stabilized in 2207. Yasmin Sayegh and Tomas Keene-Cole in 2208. Amari Adebayo, Lucien Moreau, and Keiko Tanaka in 2209. Asha Said completed the cohort in 2210 after suspending her treatment twice to revise its effects on pressure adaptation.

No two procedures were identical.

The public called them the Twelve anyway, counting around Elian when convenient and including him when a number sounded less important than a miracle.

Mara Ko kept the official count.

She was thirty-one when North Atlas appointed her recording secretary to the first long-horizon conference. Her grandmother Dema had once collapsed at the Inter-City Council after a gate conditioned water on registration. Mara had inherited the story, the settlement's distrust of city forms, and a talent for writing minutes that made powerful people recognize their own evasions.

She arranged thirteen chairs in the North Atlas council room.

Twelve stood behind city nameplates. Elian's stood behind a card reading FIRST IMMORTAL.

He moved it.

"Where?" Mara asked.

"No separate rank."

"Morrow already has Dr. Mirek."

"I am aware."

"Then where should the record place you?"

"Alphabetically."

"By city or surname?"

Elian looked at the table.

"You see the problem," Mara said.

The public gallery filled before dawn.

Families occupied the first rows. The Sayeghs arrived with archive staff, cousins, judges, and three children instructed not to ask Yasmin whether she remembered the Exchange. The Adebayo delegation brought seed gifts. The Keene-Cole family brought a railway whistle that had belonged to Tomas's great-grandmother. Al-Khatib relatives divided themselves between clerical and technical dress and then denied doing so deliberately.

The Immortals had been stable for a decade or less.

Their families had already begun arranging access.

Mara watched a Bell-Reyes cousin turn away a legal petitioner because Noa was eating breakfast. A Mirek nephew distributed printed copies of Anika's infrastructure notes with the family foundation's seal added to the cover. Two Volkov-Rao branches argued over which one had the right to sit behind Farid.

No law granted these families authority.

No law made proximity meaningless.

Leena Park sat in the second row with the Civic Equalists.

At sixty-eight, she walked with a brace and carried the same sign she had held outside Elian's consent hearing. The words had been repaired twice.

*ONE BIOREACTOR. HOW MANY LIVERS?*

Mara approached her before the session.

"Signs are permitted, but you cannot block the aisle."

"The aisle is wider for them."

"It is a security buffer."

"That's a wider aisle with a uniform."

Mara measured the space. Leena was correct by half a meter.

She moved the barrier.

The conference began with atmosphere.

Lucien Moreau presented a century-scale repair model combining particulate removal, carbon management, restored wetlands, high-altitude monitoring, and restricted industrial zones. His projections showed breathable stability expanding across most inhabited regions.

Farid objected before Lucien finished.

"Your mineral demand assumes Caspian opens four extraction districts."

"Your own capacity report says six are possible."

"Possible is not available."

"The atmosphere does not negotiate."

"Miners do."

Samira added ocean chemistry to the display. "And every mineral stream has runoff."

Lucien said, "Which treatment handles."

Asha Said looked up from the marine projections. "Treatment where?"

"At source."

"There are eight thousand sources."

"Then prioritize by load."

"That means coastal communities drink the delay."

Mateo Quispe Alvarez enlarged the cryosphere layer. "If northern albedo continues recovering this slowly, ocean circulation shifts before your atmospheric gains stabilize."

"You want to manufacture ice," Farid said.

"I want to stop manufacturing heat."

Amari Adebayo had not spoken. She examined the land-use map.

Yasmin noticed. "What?"

"All of you are using empty land."

Lucien said, "The marked zones are below settlement threshold."

"Below whose threshold?"

Mara recorded the question.

The conference had been called because city governments wanted one plan. Instead, thirteen people with longer prospective lives made disagreement longer too.

Anika proposed that every recommendation include maintenance, labor, material, displacement, and failure estimates.

Nkiru added health burdens.

Noa added personhood and consent review.

Tomas added corridor access.

Keiko added continuous warning and local shutdown authority.

Elian listened until the proposal had become a government.

"No," he said.

The room quieted.

"We are not building an executive."

Lucien replied, "No executive can hold a two-hundred-year project."

"Then the project must survive different executives."

"That is the same problem with elections between it."

"Those elections are the point."

Farid folded his arms. "You volunteered for permanent biology and discovered a love of temporary planning."

"I discovered people listening when I speak softly."

Leena raised her sign.

The city delegates met separately that afternoon. They proposed a formal Council of Continuity with one immortal representative attached to each city delegation and Elian as independent chair. Recommendations would enter the legislative calendar automatically. Emergency findings could compel a vote.

The proposal granted no direct command.

It still let thirteen unelected people decide what elected governments had to discuss.

Half the Immortals supported it.

Yasmin opposed automatic scheduling because it could bury legislatures beneath long-horizon warnings. Anika supported it because ignored maintenance killed people before politics admitted a system existed. Nkiru argued public-health findings needed a guaranteed hearing. Asha asked whether marine communities outside the cities would be heard at all. Tomas wanted corridor guilds included. Noa objected that longevity was not a qualification in itself.

"Then why are you here?" Lucien asked.

"Because you keep trying to turn survival into jurisdiction."

The families in the gallery reacted before the Immortals did.

Mara heard Bell-Reyes descendants whisper that Noa had insulted the family. A Moreau nephew told a reporter that Lucien had defended the Council against anti-scientific obstruction. Neither statement resembled the exchange.

The Houses were beginning as interpretations.

The final charter created the Quiet Council.

It could publish assessments, request records, conduct historical and scientific review, and testify before any city legislature willing to hear it. It possessed no compulsory agenda, command, vote, or veto. Every formal opinion required published evidence, named authors, and recorded dissent.

The cities adopted the charter because it was weaker than they wanted and stronger than opponents wanted.

Leena called it permanent unequal access.

She was not wrong.

At the first public session, Mara placed thirteen identical nameplates on the table in alphabetical order.

No city seals.

No title for Elian.

Families filled the gallery. Critics filled the aisle. A cluster of marked route ravens occupied the upper rail without asking permission.

Mara called the meeting to order.

"Who speaks first?" a reporter asked.

Thirteen Immortals looked at one another.

Samira said, "None of us should."

The line appeared on memorial walls, school texts, family banners, and Quiet Council stationery for centuries.

Mara's minutes preserved what followed.

Farid replied, "Someone has to."

## Chapter 12 - Reforestation

### 2230-2232

The first forest required the removal of a town.

Red Orchard had no red trees and no orchard. The name came from a pre-Exchange produce depot whose painted sign survived longer than the farms it once served. Four thousand people lived there by 2230. They repaired corridor vehicles, processed fungal grain, kept goats on the old highway median, and buried their dead on a hill above the river.

Amari Adebayo's watershed plan placed a forest across all of it.

"Not a park," she told the town assembly. "A functioning headwater system."

A man near the front said, "Does it function with us here?"

"Not at this density."

"Then say no."

"No."

The direct answer did not make the room kinder.

Amari stood before a projected map showing soil depth, water flow, contamination, projected canopy, firebreaks, wildlife corridors, and the boundaries of a future forest none of the mortal adults present would see mature.

"Your workshops can relocate to the east corridor," she said. "Housing is funded. Graves can remain under protected access."

An older woman rose.

"Protected by whom?"

"The restoration authority."

"The people moving us."

"The watershed feeds eleven settlements."

"We feed three."

"Both are true."

"Only one gets a forest."

Amari had expected anger. She had built consultation into the plan, published the models, funded relocation, preserved local employment, and rejected proposals that would have moved twice as many people.

She had mistaken reduced harm for consent.

Outside the assembly hall, House Adebayo volunteers distributed seed packets bearing Amari's name.

She had not approved the label.

She also had not ordered them removed.

Her son Kofi was thirty-seven and walked with a cane after an old corridor injury. He sat beneath the surviving depot sign while children crowded around his mother.

Amari appeared thirty-four.

Kofi had begun using a cane.

"You should go inside," she told him.

"I was inside."

"The cold is bad for your joints."

"My mother is evicting a town and checking whether I have a sweater."

"Relocating."

"There it is."

"What?"

"The word that keeps your hands clean."

Amari sat beside him.

Kofi had spent his career managing seed libraries. He had defended her work, argued with her critics, and watched people who ignored him become attentive when they learned his mother could receive their grandchildren in the same face.

"The river is dying," she said.

"Then tell them the river matters more."

"That is not what I mean."

"It is what the order means."

"I can revise the western boundary."

"Should you?"

"The soil permits it."

"I asked should."

Amari looked toward the hill where Red Orchard buried its dead.

"I don't know."

"Good. Put that on the seed packet."

The relocation began in sections.

Families moved into east-corridor housing built from standardized Morrow units. Workshops reopened. School records transferred. The new cemetery accepted future burials. The old graves remained behind a temporary fence while restoration crews removed contaminated soil around them by hand.

Some residents stayed illegally.

Restoration workers brought them food.

The authority dismissed two workers for doing so. Amari reinstated them and ordered subsistence access. The town council accused her of creating dependency to make resistance manageable.

They were not entirely wrong.

Elian Voss arrived in the spring of 2232.

Threats against the Quiet Council had increased. Most were letters, wall paint, and public performances in which actors aged while masks of the Immortals remained unchanged. A smaller number contained credible details about schedules and family homes.

Security advised Elian to address Red Orchard remotely.

He refused.

"The relocation order is already enforced by people who do not have the option to attend through a wall," he said.

Ilyana was eighty-two.

She watched him fasten an ordinary coat.

"You think going makes you equal to them."

"No."

"You think the risk makes the decision honest."

"It makes me present."

"You have always enjoyed confusing those."

He kissed her forehead.

"Cancel it," she said.

The request stopped him.

She had criticized, mocked, warned, and corrected him throughout their marriage. She had never asked him to withdraw from public work.

"Why now?"

"Because I am old enough to know dread before evidence."

"Security found no device."

"Dread isn't evidence."

"No."

"You taught everyone that and now you want to punish me with it."

Elian sat beside her.

If he canceled, opponents would say the Immortals ordered mortal displacement from protected rooms. If he attended, his security would transform the town into a controlled zone. Either choice changed other people's risk.

"I will reduce the escort," he said.

Ilyana closed her eyes.

"That is not what I asked."

He went.

The Red Orchard bombing occurred after the public meeting, when restoration crews, residents, reporters, family members, security officers, and Quiet Council staff crossed the depot yard together.

The blast did not distinguish among them.

It killed Elian Voss and forty-two mortal people. More than one hundred were injured. The depot sign fell across the road. Seed packets carrying Amari's name burned beside relocation notices.

No group established credible responsibility.

Anti-Immortal militants claimed the attack after details became public. A restoration-dispossessed organization denied involvement and accused federal security of using the dead to discredit resistance. Investigators found components that had passed through three city markets and a trigger assembled from common industrial parts.

The uncertainty survived every prosecution.

Ilyana identified Elian's body through dental records and a surgical marker.

His stabilization had not made recognition easier.

At the memorial, Morrow proposed a statue.

"No," Ilyana said.

North Atlas proposed naming the Quiet Council chamber after him.

"No."

House Voss proposed a medical foundation.

"Use his records," she said. "Not his face."

Amari visited her after the funeral.

"I should have stopped him," Amari said.

"You couldn't stop a man from attending your meeting?"

"I could have canceled it."

"Then forty-two people would still have been in that yard for other reasons."

"Maybe."

"Do not turn maybe into absolution."

Amari accepted the sentence.

Ilyana touched the back of Amari's hand. The skin looked younger than her granddaughter's.

"He thought living longer would give him more time to correct himself."

"It should have."

"It gave him more confidence that correction could come later."

The Quiet Council met with one empty chair.

The cities proposed a replacement. Medical programs had advanced. Other stable candidates existed. Morrow argued that the balance of twelve cities required two associated Immortals again or none. Civic Equalists argued that replacing Elian would prove the Council had become an office.

The surviving members voted unanimously not to fill the chair.

They removed it from the table.

The founding generation had contained thirteen people: Elian and the twelve city nominees stabilized after him. His death left twelve. Because the survivors refused a replacement, the public first called them the Twelve and, as later longevity cohorts appeared, the Last Twelve.

No later Immortal could join them. The name belonged to the original surviving witnesses: people selected when immortality had no precedent, bound to one another by Elian's empty place and by the public expectation that every opinion carried the weight of centuries. Their scarcity made disagreement personal. A dissent could not disappear into statistics. A death could not be repaired by appointment.

Their first joint opinion after Elian's death supported the Red Orchard watershed plan and condemned the coercive structure of its relocation.

Residents asked how both positions could govern anything.

They could not.

The elected regional council revised the boundary, preserved the cemetery inside a resident-controlled enclave, compensated relocated households with ownership rather than temporary housing, and continued planting.

The first trees took root in soil mixed with ash from the bombing.

A school group came to plant the outer firebreak.

The children had been born after the relocation order and knew Red Orchard as a disputed map, a memorial photograph, and the place adults lowered their voices before disagreeing. Each child carried one seedling in a fiber sleeve.

"Who are these for?" a child asked Amari.

"People downstream."

"Which people?"

"Some are not born."

The child looked offended by the assignment. "Then they cannot thank us."

A restoration worker pushed her shovel into the amended soil.

"We inherited tomorrow."

The phrase sounded prepared. It was not. She had heard her own grandmother say a similar thing about ration grain and changed the tense without noticing.

House Adebayo printed it within a month.

Amari ordered her name removed from the posters and left the sentence. Red Orchard residents painted an addition beneath one:

*We did not inherit it from you.*

The restoration authority preserved both lines.

Thereafter, *We inherited tomorrow* carried two meanings. Officials used it to justify work whose beneficiaries could not vote. Displaced families used it to ask who had claimed the right to define the inheritance. Children used it when assigned unpleasant maintenance.

"Why do I have to clean the nursery filters?"

"We inherited tomorrow."

"Tomorrow sheds too much."

The joke survived longer than the poster.

Kofi Adebayo died before they rose above the depot wall.

Amari attended his funeral looking younger than his children.

House Adebayo placed no seed packets on the grave.

Someone planted one anyway.

## Chapter 13 - Animal Uplift

### 2250

Noa Bell-Reyes arrived before the public doors opened and found the witness rail occupied.

Second-Morning had selected the highest point in the chamber, a narrow service beam above the place where human witnesses were expected to sit. The beam was not part of the approved accommodation. A temporary perch stood below it, padded, measured, and equipped with a transcription pickup. Noa had reviewed the purchase order herself.

Second-Morning ignored it.

She watched the clerks uncover their terminals and test the room microphones. Her head moved in short increments, each new sound given one black eye and then the other. The feathers at her throat lifted when the transcription system produced a tone she disliked.

"The pickup is down here," a clerk said.

"I hear it."

"It may not hear you."

Second-Morning repeated the clerk's sentence in his own voice.

The clerk looked toward Noa.

"It hears her," Noa said.

The chamber had been designed for inter-city trade arbitration. The witness table was high enough for standing humans and too high for most seated bodies. Its front panel concealed the legs, on the assumption that legs did not communicate. The public gallery used fixed seats. The air system carried citrus disinfectant through a room already crowded with the smells of wet coats, paper insulation, nervous sweat, feather oil, and the food families had brought because no one trusted the hearing schedule.

Noa placed her notes at the panel table.

Lio Bell sat two places away, turning a stylus between his fingers. He had spent more time inside the uplift laboratories than anyone on the panel. In private he spoke to researchers with an attentiveness that made them confess uncertainty. In public he became cautious, adding qualifications until his own conclusions appeared to have escaped him.

"Stop doing that," Noa said.

He looked at the stylus. "It helps."

"It tells the gallery you're afraid."

"I am afraid."

"Then keep it."

The doors opened.

Researchers entered through the side aisle. Uplift advocates entered through the center and objected to having been given an aisle as if they were one delegation. Families entered wherever their bodies fit. Bako walked with one hand beneath his daughter Temi's elbow, not supporting her weight but reminding the crowd to leave room. Two laboratory staff accompanied Olli's empty transport enclosure. Olli had not been brought. The review board had decided that attendance might distress him.

His advocate had not been permitted to decide whether absence would do the same.

Noa read that objection twice.

At the front, the laboratory director arranged his documents into three identical stacks. He used the word *subjects* in the first version of his opening statement, *participants* in the second, and *persons under review* in the copy distributed after Noa's office objected.

The change had taken four days.

Nothing in the laboratory had changed with it.

The first uplifted witness called the hearing to order before the chair could.

"You said the test was voluntary," the raven said.

The chamber transcription system rendered her name as SECOND MORNING. She had selected it from three offered translations and then complained that all three made the morning sound as if it belonged to humans first.

Dr. Lio Bell leaned toward the witness rail.

"Participation is not required for continued care."

"That is not what I said."

Second-Morning repeated his sentence in his own voice, pitch and hesitation intact.

Lio closed his eyes.

Noa Bell-Reyes watched the human members of the panel discover that quotation sounded different when the quoted person had been standing above them.

The cognition protocol had been written to prevent laboratories from destroying or retaining minds they called inconclusive. It measured symbolic transfer, future planning, self-recognition, deception, grief, tool use, consent, and the capacity to distinguish a rule from the person enforcing it.

Second-Morning had passed every section.

An uplifted capuchin named Olli had not.

Olli planned food storage, recognized his children, sabotaged locks, mourned a dead clutch-mate, and refused the picture interface. The laboratory classified the refusal as insufficient evidence of legal agency.

The hearing viewed the recording after midday.

Olli occupied a white room containing a table, a water cup, the picture board, and an examiner separated from him by transparent material. The board showed food, water, door, sleep, pain, yes, and no. Each picture had been selected through trials with other capuchins. The examiner touched the food image and offered fruit. Olli ate.

She touched the water image and offered water. Olli drank.

She touched the door image.

Nothing opened.

Olli waited.

The examiner asked him to select the image himself.

He took the board, turned it over, and struck the unmarked back against the door.

The recording classified the action as task refusal.

Second-Morning made a low sound above the gallery.

The next segment showed Olli stacking cups beneath a ceiling camera. When the tower reached the lens, the room lost its view of him. A second camera recorded him crossing to the locked enrichment cabinet and removing dried fruit with a clip he had concealed beneath his sleeping platform.

The laboratory classified that action as food-motivated problem solving.

The final segment showed staff removing the body of the capuchin Olli had lived beside for nine years. Olli remained at the door after the body disappeared. He carried bedding to the empty sleeping platform, moved it away, returned it, and slept beside the door for six nights.

The grief assessment remained inconclusive because he did not select the picture marked *sad*.

Noa watched Lio stop turning the stylus.

"Who selected the pictures?" she asked.

The examiner answered from the witness table. "The interface group."

"How many capuchins?"

"The protocol was developed across species."

"How many?"

"Two participated in preference trials."

"Did either design the mark for refusal?"

"No."

"Then what does refusal look like when it is not one of your pictures?"

The examiner looked toward the laboratory director.

Second-Morning said, "It looks like the back."

The gallery moved—not applause, not yet, but bodies recognizing that the sentence had found the place where the argument was weakest.

"He used the board," Lio said.

The director answered, "He did not use the symbolic interface."

"He used what you refused to symbolize."

Lio's voice shook. It became clearer when he stopped trying to hide that.

"The test protects claimants," Noa said.

Second-Morning shifted on the rail. "From whom?"

"From being treated as property."

"By asking the property owner whether we answer correctly."

The laboratory director objected. "The examiners are independent."

Second-Morning turned her head until one black eye held him.

"Your institute pays their building."

The sentence was ungrammatical in the official transcript. It was exact in the room.

Outside the chamber, a great-ape family waited for a school access decision. Bako, the father, could sign, speak through a throat interface, and read three languages. The city school had offered his daughter a place. Its youngest classrooms stood above a stair whose rails his hands could not close around.

"The law recognizes both of you," Noa told him.

Bako pressed his palm against the building plan.

"The law does not carry Temi downstairs."

They visited the school before the afternoon session.

The youngest classrooms occupied the second level because planners had placed older pupils nearer the exits. Human children ran the stairs in both directions, dragging bags against the rails. A raven pupil used the open center above the stairwell, which the school called flight access and the facilities office called an unresolved fall hazard.

Temi climbed without help.

She placed her hands flat on the outer rails, lifted one foot, and moved her weight carefully. The motion was practiced. Children passed around her. One slowed, waited, and then continued when Temi told him to stop staring.

At the landing, she looked back at Noa.

"I can do it."

"I see."

"He cannot."

Bako stood below. The rail narrowed where the stair turned. His hands could grip the upper section but not the turn, and the shoulder clearance forced him sideways. He could reach his daughter's classroom by the freight lift at the rear of the building. The lift required staff authorization and opened through a kitchen receiving area.

"The school offered a permanent code," Noa said.

"For me."

"Yes."

"What about the next parent?"

Noa had heard versions of the question in clinics, gates, housing offices, and personhood courts. The law offered an individual exception. The building preserved the assumption that made the exception necessary.

Temi pressed her face between the upper rails.

"If you use the kitchen, bring bread."

Bako looked up.

"This is why I need legal status. To refuse my daughter's instructions."

The joke released something in the stairwell. A teacher laughed. Noa did too. Then a delivery worker opened the rear door and asked Bako to move because the freight lift was needed for food.

The ordinary schedule closed over the joke.

Noa watched Bako step aside.

No one insulted him.

No one denied that the school welcomed his daughter.

The building still decided which of them arrived through the front.

The city called Tomorrow existed then as a set of plans, temporary streets, and promises along the Colorado Front Range. Its charter called it the first city designed from its foundation for several kinds of person. Noa's name appeared on the personhood clauses. Anika Mirek's appeared on infrastructure review. Amari Adebayo had marked animal corridors through districts planners had already described as empty.

Bako had read the charter.

"Who is the city for now?" he asked.

"Everyone."

"That is a future answer."

Noa carried the school plan back into the hearing.

She withdrew the cognition protocol.

The laboratory representatives accused her of removing the only enforceable path to recognition. Uplift advocates accused her of delaying freedom because the first law had embarrassed her. Both accusations contained enough truth to survive.

The panel worked after the gallery closed.

Food arrived in covered trays, all of it selected for human committee members. Second-Morning remained on the service beam. Bako took Temi home. Lio opened the draft on the wall and removed the word *successful* from the definition of communication.

"If no test can deny status," the city attorney said, "what prevents every trained response from becoming a personhood claim?"

"The presumption restricts the creator," Noa replied.

"That is a principle. I need administration."

"Administration is where principles go to become smaller."

"And enforceable."

The attorney expanded the proposed advocate system. "Who appoints?"

"Independent courts."

"Using whose evidence?"

"Laboratory records, household observation, behavior—"

Second-Morning dropped from the beam to the unused padded perch.

"You keep beginning with what humans collected."

Noa looked at the blank section for community evidence.

"What should it begin with?"

"The door."

"Meaning refusal?"

"Meaning outside."

The attorney rubbed both eyes. "I cannot write *the door* into a constitutional standard."

Lio said, "Write that evidence includes action directed beyond the setting controlled by the creator."

The attorney looked at him.

"That I can write."

Second-Morning pecked once at the padded perch.

"Longer."

Noa allowed herself one tired breath that almost became laughter.

"That is administration."

They worked until morning. Every definition created a boundary. Every boundary produced a case that could stand just outside it. Noa had spent her career believing precision protected people from arbitrary judgment. By dawn, she understood that precision could also give arbitrariness a cleaner desk.

The revised rule began with a presumption.

Any created being demonstrating sustained preference, relationship, self-directed action, communication, or refusal could receive an independent advocate. No single human-designed test could deny status. Uncertainty restricted the creator, not the created person.

Olli received an advocate.

He never completed the picture interface.

At the first uplift graduation, Second-Morning sat above the stage and heckled the speeches. Bako's daughter Temi crossed a ramp the school had rebuilt twice. Human children, ravens, apes, and two elephants from a Memory House received records containing no owner field.

The crowd stood.

Second-Morning remained on the rail.

"Why aren't you standing?" a reporter asked.

"I am."

The line became famous because humans found it philosophical.

She had meant that she was using her feet.

Olli's advocate visited him at the laboratory the following morning.

The room contained a sleeping platform, three locked enrichment cabinets, a food hatch, and a window facing an interior corridor. Olli had learned to open the cabinet before the staff changed its latch. His refusal to use the picture board had not prevented him from arranging stolen clips into the same sequence each night.

Door. Food. Child. Outside.

The advocate, a human named Sera, placed the legal order against the glass.

"You may leave."

Olli looked at the paper, tore it in half, and pushed both pieces beneath the door.

The laboratory director folded his arms. "This is the problem."

Sera sat on the floor.

"No. The problem is that we keep bringing him paper when he keeps showing us the door."

It took four hours to arrange transport and three weeks to find a household willing to accept observation without guardianship. Bako's family offered a room in Tomorrow. Temi objected because Olli stole fruit and slept inside cupboards. Her father objected because the city described the placement as a successful integration before it had repaired the kitchen latch.

On the first school morning, Temi packed two lunches and hid hers badly enough for Olli to find it.

"That is mine," she signed.

Olli carried it to the door.

Temi looked at her father.

"He wants outside."

Bako opened it.

The legal victory became ordinary as soon as someone had to make breakfast for it.

## Chapter 14 - Terradynamics

### 2270-2290

The people who had argued over the first survival allotment were gone except Samira.

Stabilization had preserved her body, not the council around it. Talia's chair had become a museum object. Rami's corridor marks had become infrastructure. Younger water officers knew Samira as the author of rules whose original opponents survived only in minutes she could still quote.

She entered the river ceremony carrying continuity no institution had yet learned to separate from authority.

The river ceremony began in a municipal kitchen because the governor did not trust the water.

Samira al-Khatib arrived while volunteers were filling steel dispensers from sealed city tanks. Each dispenser carried the green river emblem designed for the celebration. The water inside had traveled by pipe from an older protected reservoir.

"You are serving imported water at the First Green River," Samira said.

The kitchen supervisor did not stop checking seals.

"We are serving certified water beside the river."

"The invitation says the river is certified."

"The intake is certified."

"Twelve kilometers upstream."

"Children will be present."

Samira placed her field case on the counter. The room smelled of hot bread, sterilizing steam, and the pepper volunteers had added to the root stew. Along one wall, green wrist cloth waited in folded stacks. A public printer produced labels describing the restoration in six languages. One printer line had jammed at *self-sustaining*. The volunteer clearing it had ink across both hands.

The governor entered through the service door with two aides and a camera team.

"We should move to the bank."

Samira pointed toward the dispensers. "Why are those here?"

The governor glanced at the kitchen supervisor, who kept her attention on the seals.

"Redundancy."

"Then label it."

"The ceremony celebrates confidence."

"Confidence can read."

An aide suggested that a warning would confuse families. Samira asked whether the families had been confused by two centuries of boiling instructions, contamination colors, ration limits, and treatment failures.

No one answered.

The supervisor printed new labels:

MUNICIPAL WATER. RIVER SAMPLE CERTIFIED AT INTAKE. DOWNSTREAM TEST PENDING.

The governor read them.

"That sounds less historic."

"History contains downstream."

Outside, the bank had been reinforced for the crowd. Reeds moved in water that no longer carried the chemical sheen Samira remembered from her first survey. Children leaned over the barrier until parents pulled them back by green wrists. Restoration workers stood behind the officials, still wearing mud on their boots because the program had scheduled ceremony and maintenance on the same day.

Samira recognized one of the reed crews.

"How long since the last failed bed?" she asked.

"Eleven months."

"Predation?"

"Some."

"Root disease?"

"Below threshold."

"Upstream gate release?"

The worker looked toward the governor.

"Within revised range."

The answer was technically complete and politically edited. Samira took the sample cup from her case.

The cameras moved closer.

Samira al-Khatib tasted the First Green River from a steel cup and told the cameras not to drink it.

"The public notice says potable," the governor said.

"At the municipal intake."

"We are twelve kilometers downstream."

"Water is political over shorter distances."

She poured the sample into a field analyzer.

Children waited along the bank with green cloth tied around their wrists. Their parents had brought bread, roasted roots, and jars of clear municipal water for the celebration. The river carried no warning foam. Insects moved above reeds planted by people whose names appeared on no monument.

The analyzer cleared the sample.

Samira drank.

The children cheered before the adults knew whether the gesture was scientific or ceremonial.

Terradynamics had begun as the admission that no restoration project remained local. Lucien's atmosphere changed Samira's water. Mateo's ice changed Asha's currents. Amari's forests changed rain. Farid's factories made every machine and displaced every waste stream. Keiko's sensors watched all of it.

Their shared model covered the wall of the Quiet Council chamber.

It did not show the workers' lungs until the workers stopped the air plants.

Mara Ruiz's badge alarm sounded during a routine filter change.

She was forty meters inside the high-altitude processing unit, clipped to a service rail above a chamber that removed particulate from air before releasing it across the regional flow. Wind struck the outer shell hard enough to enter through the soles of her boots. The unit did not move. Her body believed it did.

The badge flashed amber.

Mara checked the display.

CUMULATIVE EXPOSURE REVIEW.

Not evacuation. Not acute danger. Review.

Her partner on the rail, Jalen, held up his own badge. Green.

"Again?" he asked through the mask channel.

"Mine counts the contractor cycle."

"So does mine."

"You transferred in last month."

The work schedule rotated staff every twelve days. Contractors stayed eighteen because the transport cost less when crews filled the whole maintenance block. The atmospheric model used the twelve-day assumption. The badges did not.

Mara called control.

"Exposure review on Ruiz. Request exit."

"Complete the filter lock."

"Estimated?"

"Nine minutes."

Jalen looked toward the chamber. A half-seated filter would force shutdown of the entire unit and expose the replacement crew to startup discharge.

"I'll finish," he said.

"Your certification doesn't cover the lock."

"The machine does not care."

"The inquiry will."

Control repeated the order.

Mara finished the filter.

It took eleven minutes.

At the medical station, the clinician placed her badge beside five others from the same contractor team.

"No acute injury."

"I know."

"Cumulative value exceeds the planning average, not the treatment threshold."

"Which one decides tomorrow's shift?"

The clinician looked toward the contractor supervisor.

"The schedule is under review."

Mara had heard the phrase from the badge.

By evening, workers at three facilities had compared records. The public network carried atmospheric improvement in green curves and contractor exposure in separate personnel files. Mara's son helped her place both on the same screen. He was twelve and assumed anything hidden could be found by asking the right system.

"Why didn't the planet chart know?" he asked.

"The chart knew twelve days."

"You worked eighteen."

"Yes."

"Then it knew wrong."

The next morning Mara reported to the gate and did not enter.

Jalen stood beside her.

Then the next crew.

The Oxygen Strike began without a speech. Workers placed their badges on the intake table where cameras could see the different colors.

The Oxygen Strike began at three high-altitude processing facilities. Exposure limits had been averaged across a workforce that rotated less often than the model assumed. Contractors carried the extra dose. Their badges showed it. The published planetary report did not.

Lucien arrived at the strike line in a coat too elegant for the wind.

"If these units stop for eighteen days, the particulate schedule loses a season."

Mara Ruiz, a filter technician, held up her badge.

"I lose more."

"The exposure is below acute injury."

"Then you wear it."

Farid examined the replacement schedule. "We can shut one unit and reduce the others."

"That extends the program by four years," Lucien said.

Samira asked, "How old are you?"

He stared at her.

"That is not relevant."

"It is to a worker hearing four years from an Immortal."

The strike lasted eleven days. The atmospheric program lost less time than Lucien predicted and more money than Farid admitted. Contractor exposure entered the same public model as atmospheric gain.

Nothing about the change made the work safe.

It made the danger harder to hide.

On the sixth day, wind trapped everyone inside the strike shelter.

Workers, officials, Quiet Council staff, and two camera crews shared a room designed for twelve people. Condensation ran down the inner wall. Mara's union provided lentil cakes. Lucien had brought no food because his schedule assumed a return flight.

Mara gave him half of hers.

"You do not have to," he said.

"I know."

He accepted it.

Farid projected the replacement schedule above a rack of emergency masks. He removed one planned processing line, slowed two others, and marked the industrial orders that would arrive late.

"Reicing loses machinery," Mateo said through the link.

"Housing membrane loses feedstock," Farid replied.

"Which housing?"

"Three regions."

Mara leaned forward. "Put their names up."

Farid looked at her.

"If you want us to decide whether the delay is acceptable, put the people who receive it in the room."

The regions appeared beside the exposure figures. One held flood-displaced families waiting for permanent roofs. Another manufactured medical isolation units. The third had voted against the atmospheric facilities and depended on their output anyway.

The strike committee did not become less determined.

It became quieter.

Lucien asked Mara, "What threshold ends this?"

"A schedule using the days we work."

"And if that extends the program?"

"Then the program takes longer."

He glanced at the life expectancy field beside her name. Mortal. No enhancement beyond ordinary repair.

She saw him see it.

"Do not offer me your patience," she said.

Outside, wind scraped particulate against the shelter.

Lucien removed the four-year projection from his next statement.

Along the coasts, alkalinity plants restored damaged chemistry while mineral runoff injured coves outside treatment maps. Asha closed one sanctuary and found fishing families living beyond its new boundary. Mateo's reicing stabilized downstream water while mobile Arctic communities rejected routes drawn around them. Amari's forest corridors returned birds and predators to farms that had forgotten both.

The planet improved.

Improvement acquired plaintiffs.

Asha brought one of them to the Quiet Council in a container of dead shell.

The shell came from a cove outside the main treatment map. Ocean chemistry had improved across the regional current. Mineral runoff from the alkalinity plant settled where the model expected dilution. A local current trapped it against a fishing settlement instead.

The fisher who carried the sample was named Imani Daro. She placed the container in front of Samira.

"The federal report says recovery."

"At regional scale," Samira said.

"My scale is the cove."

Asha expanded the current record. The trap appeared only after local sensor data joined the model.

"We can suspend discharge," she said.

Farid answered, "The plant stops."

"Then it stops."

"Twenty million people receive delayed treatment."

Imani Daro pushed the shell container closer to him.

"How many do I need to make twenty million?"

Farid did not touch it.

The solution required a new outflow line, temporary plant reduction, and months of work. During those months, the settlement received imported fish. Children stopped learning where the local boats found safe water because the boats stopped going.

When the cove reopened, Imani took her granddaughter out before dawn.

The girl had eaten fish all her life and never watched one come from a net.

"Is it safe?" she asked.

Imani checked the public sensor, then the water color, wind, birds, and residue line on the rocks.

"The sensor says yes."

"What do you say?"

"Today."

They launched.

Years after the First Green River ceremony, Samira returned without cameras. A girl sold cups beside the bank.

"Municipal?" Samira asked.

"River."

"Tested?"

The girl pointed to a public sensor.

"It complains before my grandmother does."

Samira bought a cup.

The girl's grandmother watched from a folding chair.

"You moved us for this," she said.

Samira recognized the old coastal settlement from the relocation record.

"Yes."

"Was it worth it?"

The river moved between them.

"To whom?" Samira asked.

The grandmother nodded once.

"Better question."

It was not forgiveness.

The river no longer needed it to run green.

Mara Ruiz returned home from the Oxygen Strike with her exposure badge sealed inside an evidence pouch.

Her wife had covered the kitchen table with repair cloth because particulate dust still worked through Mara's outer layers. Their son watched from the doorway.

"Are you sick?"

"Not today."

"The broadcast said the levels were safe."

Mara set the pouch beside his school meal.

"Safe is a schedule. They used the wrong one."

During the strike, the family rationed heat because Mara's contractor housing subsidy stopped with her shift. Neighbors brought lentils, filter cartridges, and jokes about Lucien's coat. None of that appeared in the planetary delay calculation.

When the plant reopened, workers entered in pairs. They inspected badges before machinery. The first new display placed atmospheric improvement beside cumulative worker exposure.

Lucien attended the restart.

Mara handed him a spare badge.

"For me?"

"For the next time four years sounds short."

He clipped it to the elegant coat.

At the end of the shift it showed almost nothing.

The workers made him keep it anyway.

## Chapter 15 - Regional Federations

### 2300

The continental train served breakfast according to the city whose tracks it occupied.

Tomas Keene-Cole noticed because the bread changed at 06:40 without the train slowing. Lake Junction rye disappeared from the service cart. A regional flatbread replaced it with pepper paste and boiled egg. The conductor announced no border. Passenger identity displays remained unchanged. Only the smell moving through the carriage acknowledged that one political habit had yielded to another.

Across the aisle, a family unfolded three sets of documents over the meal.

The parents paid tax through the Lake Junction compact. Their marriage had been witnessed under a river-district custom recognized by North Atlas but not by the regional inheritance office. Their son had applied to an engineering school that treated the family as continental residents for tuition and foreign residents for housing.

"Which one do we show at the next inspection?" the son asked.

His mother held up the marriage record. "This one has the most stamps."

"That means the most people doubted it," his father said.

They noticed Tomas looking.

"Do you work for the line?" the mother asked.

"Sometimes."

"That is not an occupation."

"It becomes one when something stops."

The conductor entered from the forward carriage.

"Something stopped."

Tomas covered his tea.

The train began braking.

Through the window, signal gantries appeared beneath cloth ribbons. Tables crossed the right-of-way. Steam rose from cooking pots into the cold morning. People moved between the rails without the hurried fear of trespassers. They had planned to be there long enough to eat.

"Track obstruction," the conductor said.

"I see."

"The district notice calls it a protected ceremony."

"What ceremony?"

Music reached the carriage before the answer.

"Wedding."

The family across the aisle began gathering its documents.

"Do we stay?"

The conductor said, "The route is certified."

Tomas looked at the tables.

"The breakfast isn't."

Passengers laughed, but no one knew whether to stand.

Tomas Keene-Cole crossed three former borders before breakfast and was stopped by a wedding.

The continental train slowed outside a district whose council had refused the new corridor easement. Music carried across the tracks. Families had set tables beneath the signal gantries and hung woven cloth from the power poles.

"Unauthorized obstruction," the conductor said.

Tomas looked at the tables.

"It appears authorized by everyone present."

The regional compact called the corridor open. The district called it ancestral ground. Surveyors had placed the second track through a market, a burial lane, and the courtyard where two local marriage traditions exchanged witnesses.

The engineers had reduced demolition to eleven structures.

They put the number in the success column.

Ada walked him through all eleven.

The first was a storage shed whose owner wanted compensation and had already moved the contents. The second was a wall rebuilt from three earlier walls. Two structures were market stalls. One was a public kitchen. Four were houses.

The tenth was the room where marriage witnesses signed the district book.

The eleventh belonged to a woman named Sima who was feeding wedding guests from a stove older than the current city charter.

"The relocation unit offered me a better kitchen," Sima said.

"Did you inspect it?" Tomas asked.

"Yes."

"Was it better?"

"Much."

"Then why refuse?"

She struck the spoon against the pot.

"Because they kept saying better when they meant theirs."

Ada led him behind the kitchen to the burial lane. Stones carried names in several scripts. The proposed second track crossed the newest graves because older sections were protected under a preservation law written before the district joined the regional compact.

"The survey followed the law," Tomas said.

"The dead arrived in the wrong order."

He crouched beside the survey mark. It had been painted directly on one stone.

"That violates corridor practice."

"Now it becomes real?"

"Now I can stop it under the authority I have."

Ada folded her arms. "And the rest?"

Tomas stood.

"The rest requires someone with different authority."

"That is why we stopped your train."

On the tracks, the wedding couple had begun exchanging witnesses. One family used spoken declarations. The other used signed cloth passed from hand to hand. The train passengers watched through the windows. Some complained about connections. Others left the carriages to buy food.

The conductor approached Tomas.

"How long?"

"I don't know."

"That answer propagates."

"So does building across graves."

"The timetable does not have a field for that."

"It will."

The conductor looked toward the wedding.

"Before lunch?"

"Probably not."

Tomas left the train.

A councilor named Ada Flores met him beside a pot of pepper stew.

"You are late."

"Your wedding is on the railway."

"The railway is in our wedding."

"That sentence will not survive a safety review."

"Then your safety review needs better sentences."

The regional federations were supposed to eliminate the old checkpoint world. Lake Junction supplied rail law. Chicago Deep supplied public medicine and freshwater engineering. North Atlas supplied courts. Great City practices spread across continents whose smaller governments accepted connection and feared absorption in equal measure.

Ada showed Tomas the proposed detour. It added six minutes to the continental line.

"Six minutes becomes nine hours across the network," he said.

"Only if every town asks."

"Every town will."

"Then perhaps the straight line is the expensive one."

The wedding continued while they argued. Children ran along the stopped train. Passengers bought food through open windows. A violinist from Rhine Hearth joined a drum circle from the district and failed to find the rhythm twice before someone laughed and moved his chair closer.

The delay became the first Continental Music Festival by accident.

The corridor moved east.

It cost nine minutes.

The regional hearing occurred four months later in a chamber built above another railway.

Every time a freight train passed beneath it, the water glasses trembled. Tomas presented delay models. Ada presented photographs of the survey mark on the grave. Freight unions warned that local vetoes could break continental service. District councils warned that continental service could become a machine for declaring every local refusal selfish.

The chair asked Tomas whether the nine-minute detour was technically necessary.

"No."

Ada turned toward him.

"It is politically necessary."

"Define that for the engineering record."

Tomas heard Sima strike the spoon against her old pot.

"The technically shortest route crosses things the line has no authority to price."

A freight representative said, "Everything has a price."

Ada answered, "Then put your grandmother in the schedule."

The chair called for order.

The train beneath them passed before the room found it.

Freight companies cited the delay for eighty years. The district cited the surviving courtyard for longer.

At the federation hearing, Tomas defended the detour.

"An open route is a public good," a Lake Junction delegate said.

"A route is a power relationship."

"That sounds like North Atlas."

"It sounds like a man whose train was stopped by soup."

The chamber laughed.

Ada did not.

"Tell them the burial lane," she said from the gallery.

Tomas did.

Regional passports disappeared. Regional taxes, courts, accents, and rivalries did not. The same ticket carried a traveler from Lake Junction to North Atlas. It also carried Great City law into places that had never voted in either.

When the first festival train crossed the detour, Tomas stood in the aisle.

His descendants later preserved the ticket.

They did not preserve Ada's stew recipe.

Her family did.

The morning after the festival, railway crews found cooking oil on the eastern signal housing and ribbons inside a switch.

Ada's nephews cleaned both before the first train. The regional authority sent an invoice for the delay. The district council sent one back for use of the courtyard.

Tomas attended the exchange in a bakery beside the tracks.

"You won the detour," he told Ada.

"We kept the courtyard."

"That is what I said."

"No. Winning is what governments call it when they expect gratitude."

The first regular train arrived nine minutes later than the original schedule and full enough that passengers stood. It brought a nurse, three apprentices, a family returning for the wedding season, and a property assessor whose work Ada immediately disliked.

Connection did not end the dispute.

It gave the dispute more people, money, exits, and reasons to continue.

The first Immortal to bury a great-grandchild was Yasmin Sayegh.

Rana Sayegh died at eighty-seven after a short respiratory illness. She had administered local archives, raised two children, refused three House appointments, and spent the final decade of her life insisting that her great-grandmother stop correcting dates during family meals.

At the funeral, Yasmin looked younger than Rana's surviving children.

Nobody mentioned it.

The family had expected Yasmin to speak. A lectern stood beside the body. Its display already carried her name. When the memorial clerk asked for her text, Yasmin shook her head.

"No speech."

"The public feed lists you."

"Remove me."

"House Sayegh has already announced—"

"Then House Sayegh may explain."

She sat in the first family row and listened while mortals remembered a person whose entire life fit inside Yasmin's adulthood. Rana's son described her habit of annotating recipes. A former clerk recalled that she returned unsigned forms with the missing field marked in green. A neighbor described the sound of her broom against the common walkway every morning.

After the last remembrance, procedure began.

Who signed the death record?

Rana's physician had certified the body. The physician worked for a clinic partly funded by House Sayegh. The civil registrar asked whether Yasmin's archive standing created a conflict if she countersigned.

"Her daughter signs," Yasmin said.

"The daughter is an heir."

"Every available signer is related, employed, or both."

"Then we need an independent witness."

The neighbor with the broom story raised her hand.

Who inherited?

Rana had left private papers to her children, civic records to the local archive, and a century-old memory key to Yasmin. The key contained childhood recordings of people Yasmin remembered without recordings.

Rana's son asked, "Does it return to you because you gave it to her?"

"No."

"She named you."

"Then it comes to me because she chose."

"House counsel says the object originated in the foundation."

Yasmin looked toward the counsel. "House counsel may attend another funeral."

Who remembered?

The civil archive wanted a verified life summary. The House wanted to preserve Rana's family line. Her children wanted mistakes left inside the record because their mother had made them and later laughed about them. A public historian asked Yasmin to certify a disputed date from Rana's first appointment.

"I remember a different date," Yasmin said.

The historian brightened. "Then we can correct it."

"No. Record both."

"You were present."

"So was she."

The historian looked toward the body as though death had settled reliability.

Yasmin did not let it.

No one asked who would inherit Yasmin's work. No one asked who would sign when she died. Immortality turned those ordinary questions away from her and concentrated them on everyone she outlived.

After the burial, Rana's youngest great-grandchild remained beside the memorial table.

"Did you know her better than us?" the child asked.

"Longer."

"That isn't what I asked."

Yasmin touched the memory key in her pocket.

"No."

The first Immortal funeral produced no speech. Its surviving record contained signatures, inheritance objections, conflicting memories, and a note that longevity did not make one witness sufficient.

## Chapter 16 - Enhanced Children

### 2350

Hana Vale learned her daughter's likely occupation before she learned the shape of her face.

The clinic called the display a developmental forecast. Hana called it a menu because every line had a price.

The room had been designed to soften medical decisions. Fabric covered the equipment cabinets. Wooden toys from several Great Cities occupied a low shelf. The air smelled faintly of mint from a calming system Hana had not been asked to approve.

Her partner Dev sat close enough that their knees touched. He kept one hand beneath hers on the table, not restraining it and not willing to let the clinic receive her alone.

The clinic projection listed respiratory resilience, memory consolidation, stress recovery, muscle response, probable sleep requirement, and educational risk. At the bottom, a private school consortium had attached placement recommendations.

"She is not born," Hana said.

The clinician dimmed the list.

"The recommendations are automated."

"So is the insult?"

Hana and her partner Dev had requested one intervention. A Vale-line cardiomyopathy had killed three relatives before fifty. The correction package came bundled with options their public plan would not cover later: infection resistance, improved radiation repair, attention regulation.

The clinician separated the cardiac correction from the bundle.

"This is the requested intervention. The other fields are elective."

"Elective now," Hana said.

"Yes."

"What are they when the school reads them?"

"Schools cannot access treatment selection."

Hana pointed at the placement recommendations.

The clinician dimmed that part of the display too.

"The consortium uses predicted educational need."

"Predicted from what?"

"The developmental model."

"Which uses our treatment selection."

The clinician paused. The pause was professional, neither agreement nor evasion, and Hana disliked it more than a defense.

Dev expanded the respiratory line. His work moved between restored coastal districts where mold seasons still varied.

"If we wait, can she choose this later?"

"Some respiratory interventions remain possible. They will not shape development in the same way."

"Meaning less effective?"

"Meaning different."

Hana read the uncertainty figures. "You have a number for everything except what she would want."

"There is no measurement for that."

"Then why does the screen look complete?"

The clinician lowered the entire projection.

Without it, the room contained two parents, a medical worker, and a child whose future had stopped pretending to be visible.

"If we refuse now," Dev said, "she can choose some of it as an adult."

"After schools decide what the refusal means."

Their daughter, Miren, kicked against the monitor.

That evening they ate at Hana's mother's apartment beneath the Vale route maps.

Rami Vale's blue-pencil corrections had been copied across one wall. Later generations had added flood routes, restored rail, new coastlines, and family journeys. No one could identify every hand. The family called that continuity when guests asked and argument when deciding whether to move one.

Hana's mother served bean stew before asking what they had chosen.

"Cardiac only," Dev said.

She nodded.

"You disapprove?" Hana asked.

"I am deciding whether you want my opinion or my company."

"Both."

"Those are different packages."

Dev laughed. Hana stared at the route map.

"What would you choose?" she asked.

"For myself? Better lungs. Better joints. Teeth that stop breaking."

"For Miren."

Her mother set down the serving spoon.

"I would choose not to be asked after you already chose."

"That is not an answer."

"It is the one she will receive."

Hana felt the child move again. She placed both hands over the motion, protecting it from nothing she could name.

Dev cleared the bowls after dinner.

"We can return to the clinic."

"Do you want to?"

"I want her not to blame us."

"Then we need a different technology."

They kept the cardiac correction.

Before sleeping, Hana reopened the school forecast and saved it. She told herself she was preserving evidence of institutional overreach.

Years later, Miren would ask why her mother had preserved the prediction instead of letting it disappear.

Nkiru Okafor-Bhandari joined the family review by public request. She asked about ventilation before genetics, nutrition before cognition, and the parents' work schedule before predicted achievement.

"You are avoiding the question," Hana said.

"I am locating it."

Noa attended the policy hearing that followed.

The hearing arranged parents on one side of the chamber and adolescents on the other. An uplifted delegate objected before testimony began.

"You have organized authority by age and called it seating."

Clerks moved the name cards. Adults complained about losing reserved places. Several adolescents refused the new arrangement and sat together at the rear.

Miren was six and remained outside with a childcare group. Hana heard children through the wall whenever the microphones fell quiet.

Nkiru and Noa shared the witness table. Both looked like people from Hana's school texts because neither had aged since the photographs. Nkiru read the access rules as if searching for an infection chain. Noa read the definitions and marked every place where protection required classification first.

The school consortium proposed a protected enhancement field. Employers could not view it. Medical staff and educational planners could.

"A secret category is still a category," Hana said.

"Without the field, accommodation becomes guesswork," the school representative replied.

Noa read the access list. "There are four thousand educational planners."

"Across the Federation."

"That is an argument against the word protected."

The unmodified parents' association opposed public funding. Enhancement-access organizers accused them of preserving modification for wealthy families. Uplifted delegates asked why human parents were suddenly alarmed by cognition categories they had accepted for created peoples.

An unmodified father described his son's academy rejection.

"The accepted class profile favored reduced sleep need and high memory consolidation. The school says it did not require modification."

The consortium representative answered, "The profile describes educational outcomes."

"Outcomes selected before birth."

An enhancement-access organizer leaned toward her microphone. "Then make the intervention public."

"Or stop using it as admission equipment."

"You want rich families to keep it privately."

"I want my son not to compete with a purchase."

The argument moved through the room before the chair recognized either speaker. Hana watched Noa listen without trying to combine the positions into one clean principle.

The uplifted delegate requested the floor.

"Human institutions designed cognition tests for us and called the result protection. Now you have built a quieter test for your own children and discovered coercion."

No one applauded.

The absence had shape.

Second-Morning, old and nearly featherless, sent a recorded statement.

"Welcome."

Her image remained on the wall after the word ended.

The chair waited for a longer message. None came.

Noa said, "Enter the full statement."

"That was the full statement," the clerk replied.

Children laughed through the wall at something unrelated.

Miren was born with the cardiac correction and none of the elective package.

At six, she needed classroom support for attention. The school granted it after Hana permitted access to the prenatal refusal record. At nine, another child called her baseline. At eleven, Miren used the same word against a classmate whose parents had selected memory enhancement.

Hana took her home on the tram.

"Do you know what it means?"

"That she gets answers faster."

"It means you wanted to hurt her without naming anything she did."

"She called me slow."

"Was she wrong?"

Miren stared out at the restored rain.

"No."

"Then answer what she did. Not what she is."

Hana's limited disclosure became precedent. The law protected refusal, required accommodation, and retained the record necessary to enforce both.

Miren grew up to become a clinic architect. She spent her career designing rooms where patients could refuse one treatment without losing access to another.

Her prenatal record followed every license renewal.

When Miren was eleven, her class toured Tomorrow's civic fabrication shop.

Students received the same bridge problem and different interfaces. One enhanced child completed the design before the instructor finished explaining it. Temi's great-grandson tested the model by leaning his weight against it. A raven student found a camera blind spot the safety system had labeled irrelevant.

Miren needed more time.

The instructor offered an answer cue.

"Is that accommodation or help?" she asked.

"Both."

"Does the record say which?"

The instructor hesitated.

At dinner, Hana found Miren reading her own prenatal refusal through a school portal.

"You chose this."

"We chose not to choose more."

"That is still choosing."

Dev put down his cup. "Yes."

No defense followed.

Miren closed the file.

"I want my teachers to know what I need. I don't want them to know what you feared."

Hana brought that sentence to the next regional hearing. The amended rule separated present accommodation from parental prenatal reasoning.

The old record remained in the medical archive.

Protection improved.

Forgetting did not become a right.

## Chapter 17 - Immortal Concord

### 2400

The House Sayegh entrance occupied the same wall as the public records desk.

Yasmin had objected when the archive foundation proposed it. Her descendants removed the word *private* from the sign. The separate door, staff, appointment system, security review, and upholstered waiting room remained.

The public desk had twelve hard chairs.

Yasmin entered through the public side on the morning of the Concord hearing. Two petitioners recognized her and stood. A third continued arguing with the identity terminal.

"I am not deceased," the woman told it.

The terminal requested proof from an active account.

"Dead people do not have active accounts."

The terminal repeated its request.

A foundation assistant touched Yasmin's elbow.

"Your family is waiting."

"So is she."

"The chamber is holding your place."

"The chamber will survive."

The woman turned. She was older than Yasmin appeared and young enough to resent the comparison.

"Are you the archive?"

"No."

"You look like the picture."

"That is one of the archive's mistakes."

Her name was Reva Ilyin. A regional database merge had combined her medical death notice with a housing transfer and preserved both as verified events. Her local office could see the error. Federal identity required that office to correct it. The office could not alter a record it no longer owned.

Yasmin asked the clerk how many times Reva had appealed.

"Twenty-seven contacts."

Reva said, "Do not count automated replies."

The number fell to four.

House Sayegh staff emerged from the other door with Yasmin's great-grandson. He wore the foundation seal at his collar.

"The hearing schedule—"

Reva looked between the two entrances.

"Does everyone in your family get a door?"

"It exists for security," he said.

"It does not say private."

"The foundation serves the public."

Reva pointed toward the hard chairs.

"Better cushions."

The clerk covered a laugh.

Yasmin Sayegh's great-grandson used her name to move ahead of a woman carrying her own death record.

The woman had been declared dead during a regional database merge. She had traveled two days to correct it. House Sayegh staff placed her behind delegates, archive donors, and family appointments.

Yasmin found her asleep beside the hearing wall.

"How long?"

"Alive?"

"Waiting."

"Those have become the same question."

Yasmin moved her to the front.

Her great-grandson objected in private.

"There are security procedures."

"You listed her as dead."

"The regional system did."

"Our desk enforced it."

"You cannot personally reorder every queue."

"Then stop using my name on the door."

He looked wounded. He had known her all his life. She had missed his first marriage and attended his third. He managed an archive foundation whose public work was real and whose access to her was an asset no law described.

"It is our name too," he said.

"Yes."

"You speak as if we stole it."

"You inherited it."

"From you."

"From people before me."

He closed the office door. Its inner surface carried a child's faded drawing of Yasmin at a desk. He had made it when he was six. The paper had survived two office moves because Yasmin kept forgetting to decide whether preservation was affection or habit.

"People ask me for five minutes," he said. "They send children's work, appeals, histories. If I refuse, I am hoarding you. If I agree, I am selling you. You tell us to manage access and condemn the shape management takes."

"The shape is a family door beside a public queue."

"Security requires separation."

"Security does not require upholstery."

He looked toward the wall.

"You missed my first marriage."

Yasmin had attended an Al-Noor water-custody appeal that week. She could still name the settlements and not the color he had worn.

"I know."

"You attended my third."

"Yes."

"You congratulated the wrong husband."

"For eleven seconds."

"It was a long eleven seconds."

He had become physically older than her before his second marriage. Age gathered around his eyes when anger tightened them. Yasmin remembered teaching his mother archive seals and could not remember the first time he had asked to enter her office without an appointment.

"The household period remains," she said.

"One afternoon each month for fourteen branches."

"The family must decide how to share it."

"You have made intimacy a committee."

"The family made access a foundation."

Neither sentence reached the injury beneath it.

The hearing was also carrying an election nobody understood.

Samira al-Khatib had served on the continental water board for one hundred sixty years. Voters credited her with the unconditional survival allotment, common contamination scales, watershed compensation, and a thousand less visible corrections. She remembered why obsolete pipe grades remained in the code. She knew which compromise had prevented which city from leaving. When an engineer cited precedent, Samira often remembered the room in which it had been negotiated.

Her challenger, Dena Ko, was thirty.

Dena had managed flood distribution in three districts and organized water access for orbital construction workers. She had been alive for less time than Samira had spent revising one interbasin agreement.

At the first debate, the moderator asked whether Dena possessed sufficient experience.

"For what?" she asked.

"The board oversees systems serving four continents."

"I know the systems."

"Commissioner al-Khatib knows their history."

"She was allowed to become the history."

The audience shifted.

Samira leaned toward her microphone. Immortal speech had acquired a public rhythm: slower than mortal debate, precise about dates, reluctant to make a sentence that could not survive replay.

"I was elected repeatedly," she said.

Dena answered before the sentence fully ended. "By voters who knew every challenger would be compared with your additional century."

"Should experience become disqualification?"

"Does it ever stop becoming advantage?"

No one knew how to measure that.

The ballot rules imposed equal spending, equal broadcast time, and equal access to public records. They could not provide equal memory. Samira entered hearings carrying facts no archive search could reproduce because she remembered which silences had meant threat and which had meant translation delay. Dena entered homes without security, slept in flood barracks, and knew the current names residents used for systems Samira still called by former districts.

At a neighborhood meeting, a pipe worker asked Samira why she wanted another term.

"The polar transfer review will outlast one mortal term."

Dena, seated beside her, said, "Then the project needs continuity that is not one body."

"It has an archive."

"You just told voters memory is better than archives."

"Memory supplies context."

"Then write the context down."

Samira looked toward Yasmin in the witness gallery.

Yasmin did not rescue her.

Mortal voters divided for incompatible reasons. Some trusted Samira because their grandparents had. Some opposed her because their grandparents had. Younger engineers resented a vacancy that never opened. Older residents feared replacing the one official who remembered why their settlement's water claim existed. House al-Khatib funded no campaign advertising, but every public fountain bearing Samira's name advertised anyway.

The night before voting, Dena found Samira inspecting a distribution station.

"Campaign visit?" Dena asked.

"The chloride readings changed."

"They changed last month."

"The public report rounded them."

Dena opened the raw record. "You noticed from the summary?"

"I know the source."

"That is the argument for you."

"Yes."

"And the argument against you."

Samira closed the panel.

"Yes."

The election returned Samira by three percent.

Her supporters called it public trust. Dena's supporters called it accumulated incumbency. Civic Equalists called it proof that formal equality could not neutralize time. The Houses called it democratic consent and printed the result beside Samira's portrait.

At her swearing-in, Samira requested term limits.

Dena attended from the public gallery.

"After you won?" she called.

"Before the next person becomes an institution."

"You already did."

Samira accepted the correction into the official record.

The proposed limits would remove her before the polar transfer review concluded. She supported them anyway.

Yasmin attended her five-hundredth recorded funeral that morning.

She had not counted them.

The archive had.

The dead man was a mortal theologian who had spent forty years studying whether longevity changed religious obligation. He and Yasmin had argued in public, eaten together twice, and exchanged letters for eighty-one years.

His family asked her to speak.

"He wrote more about you than anyone alive," his daughter said.

"Then the funeral has enough of me."

"He considered you a friend."

"That is why I should not turn him into evidence."

Yasmin sat with the family.

The service included prayers from his Christian tradition, the Three Questions from his Celestialist work, and a silence requested by relatives who practiced neither. During the final hymn, Yasmin remembered four earlier versions he had disliked and could no longer recall which one he had chosen.

She checked the program.

Memory lost to paper.

Afterward, a child asked whether funerals felt different when a person did not expect to die.

"I expect to die," Yasmin said.

"When?"

"That is the part neither of us knows."

Religions had divided over the Last Twelve without dividing cleanly by religion.

Some Christian communities treated radical longevity as another medical preservation of life. Others warned that centuries could tempt a person to confuse continuation with resurrection. No major church agreed that stabilization made an Immortal spiritually superior, but House admirers still carried images of the Twelve as if portraits could become icons by denial.

Muslim scholars generally rejected any inference that long life granted sacred authority. Debate continued over wealth, obligation, inheritance, and whether an Immortal's indefinitely accumulated power violated duties that ordinary law had failed to constrain.

Jewish legal discussions treated Immortals as persons facing unprecedented durations of familiar obligations, not as a new category of soul. The hardest questions concerned testimony, marriage, inheritance, mourning, and how one witness's living memory should relate to texts and communities that had changed around them.

Hindu schools disagreed over whether stabilization prolonged one embodied stage, disrupted expected life transitions, or changed nothing essential about karma and self. Buddhist teachers asked whether centuries encouraged wisdom or merely extended attachment.

Celestialism designated the Immortals continuity witnesses and explicitly denied them prophetic status.

The Houses printed *continuity witness* on ceremonial banners anyway.
