Mara added the date. Temporary things acquired authority when no one remembered when they began.

It had marked failed borders, caravan routes, constitutional dissent, Solar navigation, and an address beyond Sol.

The first child to use it drew a line around the broken ship.

She held the pencil too close to its point. Mara showed her how to move her fingers back without taking it away. The child drew slowly across a map made from taped packaging sheets.

"Is that the city?" Mara asked.

The child shook her head.

"That is the part we can reach."

Beyond her line lay pressure-safe rooms no route yet connected, dark sections transmitting occasional suit tags, and open crater ground. The map did not claim those places were empty. It claimed only that a child could not walk to them.

Mara wrote the sentence beneath the map.

Above Craterheart, the new sun rose through dust.

Rescue continued beneath it.

Teams worked in cycles measured by air bottles and cutting-tool temperature. Every opening risked changing the pressure map. People waiting outside rescue sites learned the sounds: drill, pause, brace, call, listen. Silence after a call meant either concentration or grief. No one could tell which until the team emerged.

Ina Volkov's lower seam had held. Her son survived in a habitat buried nose-first beneath the crater wall. The rescue team reached him after thirty-one hours by cutting through a school storage bay.

The habitat's main hatch opened into compacted regolith. Its secondary route crossed a collapsed spin bearing. A child examining an old emergency diagram noticed that the school storage bay shared a maintenance cavity with the habitat's water return.

Cutters followed the cavity. They removed shelves, lesson kits, and the ark's boxed musical instruments. Dust entered through the first breach and turned sealant gray. The rescue crew crawled the last six meters while Ina's son tapped a torque key against pipework.

The key transmitted poorly but consistently.

He woke asking whether the seam had failed.

Cael recognized the question before the medics did. He leaned close enough for the injured man to see his mouth through the mask.

"No," Cael told him. "Everything around it did."

Ina's son closed his eyes.

"She'll ask."

"We will tell her."

The first outbound message to Sol carried casualty counts, drive failure, settlement status, and a pressure reading from the lower seam. Ina would receive facts years too late to alter them. The assembly sent them anyway.

Six hours later, exterior sensor eleven reported pressure inside a section everyone had marked lost.

The compartment lay beyond the detached habitat the engineers had seen across the crater. No corridor connected it to Craterheart. The exterior route crossed unstable wreckage and open ground. Its transponder did not answer. Thermal imaging showed no movement. The pressure could be trapped atmosphere held inside an empty room.

Or the walls could be keeping people alive beyond a failed antenna.

The rescue council met at the air-pack rack.

"Four packs for travel," the quartermaster said. "Four for entry and return. Two reserve."

"That is half the exterior stock."

"Correct."

"The clinic needs reserve for hull breach."

"Also correct."

The structural map showed a stress line above the compartment. Cutting the wrong panel could vent it. Approaching through the buried side required excavation equipment still trapped in another section.

Command classified rescue probability as low.

A woman in the waiting group held up a manifest slate. Her partner had been assigned to the lost section.

"Low is not zero."

The quartermaster answered, "Air is also not infinite."

"His air may not be either."

No one in the room opposed rescue. They opposed the cost that might make a later rescue impossible.

Cael projected the exterior route. "Three engineers, one medical unit, tether every fifteen meters. No cutting until independent pressure confirms."

"If the ground shifts?"

"Abort."

"If they hear a person?"

Cael looked toward the woman.

"Then the team decides what abort means."

The answer angered command because it surrendered authority at the exact distance where communication might fail.

Mara opened the old Sayegh rescue rule carried in the Earth archive. It required public accounting when a mission risked common life-support capacity. The rule had once sent Darius Cole toward a clinic because four infants might have existed at the end of a radio signal.

"It does not require rescue," Mara said.

"What good is it?" the captain asked.

"It requires us to say who pays if we are wrong."

The quartermaster listed the cost. Ten air packs. Two exterior patch kits. One medical unit. Four people exposed. Delayed viewport reinforcement. Reduced reserve until packs could be refilled.

The woman listed the other side.

"Twenty-three assigned residents. My partner. Six school staff. The ark music archive was stored there."

The captain said, "Music is not rescue weight."

"Then ignore it. I included it so you would know what else you are willing to name."

The council voted.

The mission passed by two.

Nobody cheered. The quartermaster issued the packs personally and made every team member sign the reserve reduction. The woman helped inspect glove seals though she was not permitted outside.

At the hatch, Cael found Mara carrying a route slate.

"Navigation needs you inside."

"The route is navigation."

"You are injured."

"So are you."

"I am not going."

Mara looked at his splinted arm.

"That is unexpectedly sensible."

"I voted against."

"I know."

"I still plotted the safest route."

"I know that too."

Four figures left the lock and moved into the red dust. At fifteen meters they fixed the first tether. At thirty, the wreck blocked the viewport. Craterheart followed them through suit telemetry until terrain reduced the signal to four breathing traces.

No voice came from the lost section.

No movement appeared.

They went anyway.

Families gathered outside the casualty desk with manifest numbers, copy-line identifiers, clutch records, and photographs that no longer matched injured faces. Mara worked the desk when navigation could spare her. The archive knew where everyone had been assigned. Impact had made assignment useless.

People arrived carrying proof of relationship because old institutions had trained them to expect doubt. They brought marriage registrations, household votes, guardianship judgments, shared-medication lists, and messages sent before launch. Mara stopped asking for documents unless two claims conflicted.

"Name and last known place," she told each new arrival.

"Don't you need to know who I am?"

"You came. That is enough to begin looking."

A woman asked for her wife.

The manifest showed the wife in an aquatic civic volume whose surviving residents had not completed identification.

"Is she alive?"

"I don't know."

"Then why do you have all those records?"

Mara looked at the map.

"To know where we failed to know."

The woman stayed beside the desk until an aquatic delegate arrived twelve hours later.

Volunteers offered her broth. She held the cup until it cooled. Every time the corridor membrane opened, she stood.

Her wife had survived.

The aquatic delegate rendered the confirmation first in color, then words. The woman understood the color and collapsed before the translation finished.

Three other families waiting there received different answers.

The kitchen lights lowered three times that shift.

The first communal kitchen opened in a freight bay because its warming coils still worked. Belt colonists cooked ration paste with contraband spice. Venusian residents measured every gram of water. A Jovian child complained that the walls were too thin for sleep and was told the new planet had no radiation weather.

The contraband spice had crossed the manifest review inside a medical powder tin. Its owner refused to say which inspector had known. A pinch changed the smell of the entire bay. People followed it from two corridors away and waited with bowls made from cut storage liners.

The Venusian water keeper stood over the pot.

"Steam is loss."

"Steam is why people know dinner exists," the Belt cook said.

They compromised on a lid opened for ten seconds at each serving.

"Then why are we underground?"

"Because the ship fell on us."

The child considered this.

"Worse weather."

The child carried her bowl beneath a table and ate there. Two other children joined her. By evening, the space had a blanket wall and a sign reading JUPITER, though nothing about its gravity was correct.

The first school lesson met beside the machine shop. The teacher asked children to draw the settlement. Every map looked different because no corridor network was complete.

Machine noise interrupted each instruction. The teacher raised a hand whenever the saw started, and the children raised theirs with her until speech became possible again. No one had allocated desks. They knelt on insulation mats and drew against tool cases.

One drew the wreck as a person.

The drive spine became legs. The rotating habitats became arms. The crater became a blanket.

One drew only doors.

Safe doors were green. Jammed doors were orange. Doors behind which someone had died were left uncolored.

One marked the clinic, kitchen, toilets, and the compartment where her brother remained missing.

She drew the missing compartment larger than the rest of the wreck.

The teacher laid all the maps together and noticed that none contained Earth.

They contained Craterheart, the crater rim, the new sun, detached habitats, doors, family sleeping places, the casualty desk, and routes children were forbidden to enter. One child drew the *Deliverance* moving across the page before breaking into the ground.

No child drew the world it had left.

"Where is Earth?" the teacher asked.

A Saturnian child pointed upward.

"That is outside."

"Earth is outside."

"Outside the map?"

"Yes."

The teacher opened the ark's standard classroom projection. Earth appeared as a blue sphere beside the Solar system route. Several children recognized the image. None changed their drawing.

"This is where the mission began," she said.

"Charon," another child corrected.

"Before Charon."

"My mother began on Venus."

"Mine in the Belt."

"Grandfather Cael says routes begin where somebody starts recording."

The teacher turned off the projection.

Adults had packed Earth as an origin. The first generation in Craterheart understood it as a collection of other people's beginnings.

One child added a small arrow at the edge of her map and wrote SOL. It pointed behind the page.

"Is Earth there?" the teacher asked.

"Everything there."

"That is a long way."

"The paper is small."

The maps were not evidence that Earth had been forgotten. They were evidence that geography began with places a child might need to reach: food, air, family, toilets, medicine, and the door that opened toward morning.

The emergency assembly wanted one official map for ration delivery.

The teacher sent all of them.

The ration office objected that conflicting maps could not guide delivery.

"Neither can a false agreement," the teacher replied.

Workers combined the maps layer by layer. Children's routes exposed passages adults had dismissed as too small. The door map showed which official paths could not be opened by a person with limited strength. The missing-compartment map kept a search area visible after command had categorized it as low probability.

Mara added the reachable boundary from the blue-pencil child.

The official map began as disagreement.

Mara pinned the composite beside the warped pressure door. People corrected it in pencil, grease, colored touch marks, and Aster overlays. No notation was declared standard during the first week. The map remained legible because everyone needed it more than they needed ownership of it.

Cael found the porridge pan beneath a collapsed storage rack.

Its handle had bent against a cargo brace. One side carried a black mark from the Neptunian kitchen where his mother had wrapped it in an old shirt and told him every pan burned porridge if the cook walked away.

The three Saturnian children from his manifest household followed him into the salvage bay.

"Is that the family pan?" the youngest asked.

"One of them."

"How many family pans are there?"

"Every family believes one pan explains it."

"Does this one?"

Cael tested the handle.

"It explains breakfast."

They carried it to the freight-bay kitchen. The Venusian water keeper approved one cup. A Belt cook contributed grain and the smallest legal pinch of contraband spice. The warming coil heated unevenly, so Cael moved the pan by hand while the children argued over who had been assigned stirring.

"Grandfather Cael gets first turn," one said.

"He is not our grandfather."

"We voted."

"That does not change his ancestry."

Cael stirred the bottom before it caught.

"It changed the seating plan."

The oldest child studied him.

"Are we Vales now?"

"No."

"Then why do you have us?"

The manifest had created the household because mass, age, skills, and care schedules balanced. No marriage, ancestry, city, or prior friendship connected them. Impact had removed two adults originally assigned to it. The children still used the voted title because correcting it would have made the absence more visible at every meal.

"Because we are here," Cael said.

"That is not a family rule."

"It was the first one."

He served the porridge.

The pan sat among bowls cut from storage liners. Cael projected the old Vale route wall onto a freight partition: Rami's caravan corrections, Hana's coast, Imani's postal paths, his mother's Saturn transfers, his own line toward Neptune, Acheron, and the route the *Deliverance* had intended to complete.

The final line ended in the wrong place.

One child pointed toward it.

"Is that us?"

"It was supposed to be."

"Then correct it."

Cael looked toward Mara's composite map beside the door.

"The blue pencil is in navigation."

"Use another color."

Vale family practice had turned one child's pencil into a relic, a thousand copied maps, a postal symbol, and an expectation that every descendant would understand routes. Cael had spent much of his life resenting the inheritance while carrying it farther than any Vale before him.

He handed the child a grease marker.

She drew from the broken route into the crater, down the lower ladder, past the kitchen, and beneath the table labeled JUPITER. Her line ignored scale. It reached every place she needed.

"Wrong color," her sibling said.

"New world."

The third child added the exterior tether toward the lost section.

"People are there."

"Maybe," Cael said.

"Then the route goes."

He thought of Rami marking uncertainty instead of erasing it, Imani carrying messages through wars, and a family habit of believing a better line could repair political separation.

The habit had often been wrong.

The child marked the route anyway and added a question sign at its end.

Cael copied the correction into the household layer without changing her hand.

No child at the table had been born a Vale. All three had inherited the unfinished route.

The youngest tasted the porridge after it had cooled.

"This isn't Saturn porridge."

"No," Cael said.

"Is it Vale porridge?"

"Also no."

The Belt cook leaned over from the next table.

"Crater porridge."

The Venusian water keeper objected that the settlement had existed for seven days and could not already claim a cuisine.

"It has a pot," the cook said.

"It has one pot."

"Founding quantity."

The children adopted the name before either adult approved it. By the next meal, three kitchens served different mixtures under the same title and argued over which had been first.

Civilization resumed by making tradition faster than evidence could stop it.

On the seventh day, the pressure door bearing the first law finally closed.

The repair crew could have forced it earlier by cutting away the written panel. They waited until the clerk had copied every word, signature, objection, translation error, and smear of blood. During those seven days, someone always had to stand beside the gap and warn children away from the damaged frame.

When the wheel completed its turn, the seal indicator lit green. People in the freight chamber applauded the pressure reading.

The repair chief tested the frame with Rhea Mirek's hammer.

The sound was wrong at the lower hinge.

They opened the door, reset the bearing, and closed it again.

The second sound held.

Workers removed the written panel for the archive and replaced it with a functioning seal.

The panel required six people to carry it uphill. It was heavier than the memorial stone removed from the *Deliverance* manifest. Nobody proposed leaving it behind.

The law survived.

The door did its more immediate work.

Air stopped escaping.

On the other side, the new sun rose again. This time no one called it the first sunrise. A kitchen shift complained that dawn glare made the water ledger hard to read. Children argued over whose map should hang nearest the school. Cael searched stores for the pan his mother had packed three centuries before.

Mara returned to navigation.

The ship could not fly. The city could breathe. Between those facts lay every decision they had not yet made.
